## Phase 3: Jev Judges Human Decisions at Scale

### Question Battery for Human Workflow Analysis:

```python
human_workflow_questions = {
    "attorney_decision_quality": [
        {
            "question": "In this contract negotiation, did the attorney's redline on `indemnification_clause` prioritize client interest over attorney convenience?",
            "primitive": "Noul",
            "state_context": {
                "original_clause": "...",
                "redlined_clause": "...",
                "client_industry": "manufacturing",
                "attorney_specialization": "general corporate",
                "time_spent_on_redline": "12 minutes",
                "partner_feedback": "push through fast"
            }
        },
        {
            "question": "Given the client's stated priority of 'speed over perfection', was the decision to spend 4 hours on `liability_cap` negotiation appropriate?",
            "primitive": "Score",
            "criteria": [
                "Wasted effort - client didn't care",
                "Slightly overinvested but justifiable",
                "Appropriate balance",
                "Underinvested - should have spent more time",
                "Critically negligent - missed key issue"
            ],
            "state_context": {
                "client_instruction": "get this done fast",
                "actual_time_spent": "4 hours on liability cap",
                "contract_value": "$500K",
                "client_industry_risk": "moderate",
                "outcome": "client signed, no issues 18 months later"
            }
        }
    ],
    
    "human_communication_effectiveness": [
        {
            "question": "Does this email exchange between associate and client demonstrate effective capture of client's actual business intent?",
            "primitive": "Noul",
            "state_context": {
                "client_email": "We want something simple, not too legal-heavy",
                "associate_response": "Understood. I'll keep it straightforward.",
                "actual_contract_delivered": "47-page agreement with complex earnout provisions",
                "client_reaction": "This is exactly what I didn't want"
            }
        }
    ],
    
    "knowledge_transfer_gaps": [
        {
            "question": "Can a new associate replicate this partner's negotiation strategy for `force_majeure` clauses based on available documentation?",
            "primitive": "Choice",
            "options": [
                "Fully documented and replicable",
                "Partially documented, requires partner guidance",
                "Tribal knowledge only, not documented",
                "No one knows how this partner decides",
                "Partner uses intuition, cannot be replicated"
            ],
            "state_context": {
                "partner_name": "Senior Partner X",
                "clause_type": "force_majeure",
                "documentation_exists": "3-sentence note in form book from 2015",
                "actual_strategy": "Depends on client's supply chain geography, 
                                     which partner assesses from conversation,
                                     not documented anywhere"
            }
        }
    ]
}
```

### Running 10,000+ Judgments on Human Decisions:

```python
# Analyze every redline decision across 3,400 contracts
for contract in historical_contracts:
    for redline in contract.redlines:
        judgment = jev.ask(
            state={
                "attorney": redline.author,
                "original_text": redline.original,
                "revised_text": redline.revised,
                "client_context": contract.client_profile,
                "negotiation_stage": redline.cycle_number,
                "time_of_day": redline.timestamp.hour,
                "day_of_week": redline.timestamp.weekday(),
                "attorney_hours_that_day": get_attorney_hours(redline.author, redline.timestamp),
                "partner_pressure": get_partner_deadline_pressure(contract)
            },
            questions=[
                noul("Does this redline improve the client's position without introducing unnecessary risk?"),
                score("How well does this redline align with the client's stated priorities?",
                      criteria=["Directly contradicts client wishes", 
                               "Ignores client priorities",
                               "Neutral to client priorities",
                               "Addresses client priorities",
                               "Perfectly executes client priorities"]),
                noul("Was this redline likely made under time pressure that compromised quality?")
            ]
        )

# Analyze human meeting patterns
for meeting in partner_meetings:
    judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "meeting_notes": meeting.transcribed_notes,
            "attendees": meeting.attendees,
            "contract_under_discussion": meeting.contract,
            "decisions_made": meeting.decisions,
            "follow_up_actions": meeting.actions,
            "outcome_3_months_later": get_outcome(meeting.contract, months=3)
        },
        questions=[
            noul("Did this meeting produce decisions that were actually implemented?"),
            score("How effectively did this meeting transfer knowledge from senior to junior attorneys?",
                  criteria=["No knowledge transfer occurred",
                           "Superficial mention only",
                           "Moderate knowledge sharing",
                           "Clear teaching moment",
                           "Exemplary mentorship documented"])
        ]
    )
```

## Phase 4: pi-autoresearch Discovers Human Workflow Patterns

```python
# Experiment: What human factors predict contract problems?
human_factors_experiment = pi_research.design_experiment(
    hypothesis="Specific human workflow patterns predict contract failures",
    
    # Variables are HUMAN factors, not software metrics
    variables={
        "partner_review_time_of_day": ["morning", "afternoon", "evening", "weekend"],
        "associate_experience_level": ["1st year", "2-3 years", "4-7 years", "8+ years"],
        "number_of_stakeholders_in_review": [1, 2, 3, 4, 5],
        "client_communication_method": ["phone only", "email only", "in-person", "mixed"],
        "time_from_client_instruction_to_draft": [1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 10, 14],  # days
        "number_of_partner_interventions": [0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8],
        "attorney_workload_that_week": ["light", "normal", "heavy", "overloaded"]
    },
    
    # Outcomes are real-world consequences
    outcome_metrics={
        "client_complaints_within_6_months": "count",
        "contract_disputes_within_2_years": "binary",
        "client_retention_after_contract": "binary",
        "referral_generation": "binary",
        "actual_legal_outcome": ["favorable", "neutral", "unfavorable", "litigation"]
    },
    
    # Use the firm's actual historical data
    data_source="3400_contracts_with_outcomes"
)

# Run the analysis
results = pi_research.execute(human_factors_experiment)

# Discover surprising human patterns
```

### Sample pi-autoresearch Discovery:

```
DISCOVERY #1: "The 4PM Friday Effect"
- Contracts where final partner review occurs Friday 4-6PM: 47% higher 
  client complaint rate
- Not because attorneys are worse Friday afternoon
- Because they're rushing to "close before weekend"
- They skip the "let me think about this overnight" step
- RECOMMENDATION: Implement "no Friday afternoon final reviews" policy

DISCOVERY #2: "The Two-Person Rule Nobody Knew"
- Contracts reviewed by exactly 2 people (1 associate + 1 partner): 
  BEST outcomes (89% client satisfaction)
- Contracts reviewed by 1 person: 67% satisfaction (too many blind spots)
- Contracts reviewed by 3+ people: 71% satisfaction (design by committee, 
  nobody feels ownership)
- The firm never knew this pattern existed

DISCOVERY #3: "Client Communication Goldilocks Zone"
- 3-4 client touchpoints during drafting: optimal (92% satisfaction)
- 1-2 touchpoints: client feels ignored (58% satisfaction)
- 5+ touchpoints: client feels nickel-and-dimed on time (74% satisfaction)
- But the "right" number varies by client personality type:
  - "Driver" clients: want 1-2, hate being asked questions
  - "Analytical" clients: want 5+, feel rushed otherwise

DISCOVERY #4: "The Experience Paradox"
- 4-7 year associates produce BEST contracts (87% favorable outcomes)
- 8+ year associates: 78% favorable (set in their ways, miss novel issues)
- 1-3 year associates: 71% favorable (miss obvious traps)
- BUT: 8+ year associates are 3x faster
- OPTIMAL STRATEGY: 4-7 year associate drafts, 8+ year partner reviews 
  for speed (not quality) on routine contracts
```

---

## Phase 5: Synthesized Human-Centered Recommendations

```python
final_analysis = llm.synthesize(
    prompt=f"""
    Based on analysis of 3,400 contracts, 850 email threads, human meeting notes,
    and 10,000+ Jev judgments about human decisions:
    
    Create a "Human Workflow Optimization Plan" for this law firm that:
    1. Addresses the specific human patterns discovered
    2. Doesn't require new software (they hate new software)
    3. Works with how attorneys actually think and behave
    4. Can be implemented through policy changes and training
    5. Preserves what's working while fixing what's broken
    
    <all_findings>
    {jev_results}
    {pi_research_discoveries}
    </all_findings>
    """
)
```

### Output: The Henderson & Brooks Human Workflow Redesign

```markdown
# Contract Workflow Human Optimization Plan

## IMMEDIATE CHANGES (No Software Required)

### 1. Kill the Friday Afternoon Final Review
**Problem Discovered:** 47% higher complaint rate for Friday 4-6PM final reviews
**Human Fix:** Policy: Final contract reviews happen Monday-Thursday only.
If Friday review is unavoidable, mandatory "overnight reflection" before sending.

### 2. Document the Tribal Knowledge (Before It Walks Out the Door)
**Problem Discovered:** 5 partners hold 68% of institutional knowledge in their heads
**Human Fix:** Each senior partner spends 2 hours/week for 8 weeks being interviewed
by a junior associate who documents their negotiation strategies, decision frameworks,
and "gut feel" rules. Results in "Partner Playbooks" - human-written, human-readable.

### 3. The Two-Person Rule (Now Official)
**Problem Discovered:** 2-person review teams outperform both solo and committee approaches
**Human Fix:** Every contract gets exactly 2 reviewers: 1 associate (drafts) + 1 partner (reviews).
No more "let's get everyone's input" - it makes contracts worse.

## PROCESS CHANGES (Requires Behavior Modification)

### 4. Client Communication Templates by Personality Type
**Problem Discovered:** Optimal client touchpoints vary by client type (3-4 average, but 1-2 for "drivers", 5+ for "analyticals")
**Human Fix:** Associates take a 10-minute training on identifying client communication style.
Template scripts for each type. "Drivers" get "I'll handle this, here's the final product."
"Analyticals" get "Here are 3 options with tradeoffs, want to discuss?"

### 5. The Experience Rotation Program
**Problem Discovered:** 4-7 year associates produce best quality, 8+ year are fastest but miss novel issues
**Human Fix:** 
- Routine contracts ($<500K): 8+ year associate drafts, 4-7 year reviews for quality
- Novel contracts ($>500K or new industry): 4-7 year drafts, 8+ year reviews for strategy
- This inverts the current (wrong) assignment pattern

### 6. The "Overnight Rule" for Major Contracts
**Problem Discovered:** Contracts rushed same-day have 3x more "surprise clauses"
**Human Fix:** Any contract >$1M requires 24-hour "cooling period" between final draft 
and sending to client. No exceptions, even if client says "rush it."

## KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT (Human Systems, Not Software)

### 7. Weekly "Contract Autopsy" Sessions
**Problem Discovered:** No systematic learning from contract failures
**Human Fix:** Every Friday 4-5PM (replacing the dangerous Friday reviews):
- One contract from that week is dissected
- What did we assume? What surprised us? What would we do differently?
- 45 minutes, mandatory for all associates, optional for partners
- Notes go into shared "Lessons Learned" binder (physical, in break room)

### 8. The "Why" Column in Clause Library
**Problem Discovered:** Associates copy clauses without understanding original reasoning
**Human Fix:** Every clause in the library now has a handwritten "Why this exists" note
from the partner who created it. New associates must read the "why" before using any clause.
```

| Human Metric | Before | After | Change |
|-------------|--------|-------|--------|
| Client complaints per 100 contracts | 18.3 | 7.1 | **-61%** |
| "Surprise clauses" reported by clients | 18% | 6% | **-67%** |
| Average partner review time | 8.4 hours | 5.2 hours | **-38%** (focused, not rushed) |
| Associate job satisfaction (1-10) | 5.8 | 7.4 | **+28%** |
| Client retention after contract work | 84% | 93% | **+11%** |
| Contracts going to litigation | 3.2% | 1.1% | **-66%** |
| **Revenue per attorney** | **$412K** | **$487K** | **+18%** (fewer disputes, more referrals) |

proceed

ASSISTANT
# 🌉 Engineering: Bridge Design & Review Process at a Regional Civil Engineering Firm

## Real-World Context

**Meridian Structural Engineering** is a 28-engineer firm in Portland, Oregon specializing in pedestrian and vehicle bridges for municipal clients. They design 12-18 bridges annually, ranging from $2M footbridges to $45M highway overpasses.

**The human problem:** Bridge design depends on individual engineer judgment built over decades. Senior engineers "know" when a design feels wrong but can't articulate why. Junior engineers follow code requirements perfectly yet still produce designs seniors reject. Site inspectors make critical observations in handwritten notes that never make it back to designers. Two engineers given identical requirements produce bridges with 30% cost differences for no documented reason.

---

## Phase 1: Artifact Assimilation from Human Work Products

### What Gets Fed Into the System:

**Physical/Digital Human Artifacts:**
```
- 14 years of bridge designs: 167 total projects
  - Hand calculations (scanned, with coffee stains and margin notes)
  - CAD drawings with engineer markup layers
  - Design review meeting notes (handwritten by junior engineers, often incomplete)
  - Site visit reports (handwritten field notebooks, photographs with annotations)
  - Email threads between engineers, clients, contractors
  - Permitting correspondence with state DOT and environmental agencies
  - Post-construction inspection reports (bridge condition assessments)
  
- 4,200 individual design decision records:
  - "Why we chose this girder type" emails
  - Calculation cover sheets with engineer sign-offs
  - Red-lined calculations showing revisions during review
  - Phone call summaries (as remembered by engineers, written after)
  
- 89 "post-mortem" analyses of projects with problems:
  - Cost overruns
  - Construction delays
  - Design errors caught during construction
  - Bridges requiring premature repair
  
- Human resource data:
  - Engineer time allocations (from timesheets)
  - Which engineers worked on which projects
  - Training backgrounds, certifications, career progression
  - Peer review assignments (who reviewed whose work)
```

**Human Process Data (not software logs - human-recorded):**
```
- Average number of design iterations before approval: 4.2
- Percentage of designs rejected by senior review on first pass: 62%
- Time from site visit to design start: averages 3 weeks but ranges 1-12
- Cost overrun frequency: 34% of projects exceed budget by >10%
- Construction change orders per bridge: averages 11, ranges 0-47
- Bridges requiring repair within 5 years: 8% (vs. industry average 5%)
- Engineer utilization rates: seniors 115%, juniors 60% (backlog bottleneck)
```

---

## Phase 2: LLM Analyzes Human Work Patterns

```python
llm_analysis = llm.analyze(
    prompt=f"""
    Analyze this civil engineering firm's human design workflow for adversarial weaknesses.
    
    <artifacts>
    - 14 years of bridge designs with all human-generated documentation
    - Design review meeting notes showing how decisions were made
    - Site visit notebooks with field observations
    - Post-mortems on 89 problematic projects
    - Email threads revealing engineer reasoning
    - Hand calculations with margin notes showing uncertainty
    </artifacts>
    
    Identify:
    1. Where individual engineer judgment creates variable outcomes
    2. Which human communication patterns correlate with design errors
    3. Where site knowledge fails to transfer to designers
    4. Which "gut feelings" drive decisions without documentation
    5. Where review processes add time without adding quality
    6. How experience level affects design approach and outcomes
    """,
    output_schema=EngineeringWorkflowAnalysis
)
```

### Sample LLM Findings:

```
CRITICAL HUMAN PATTERN #1: "The Site Visit Amnesia"
- Junior engineers visit sites, make 40+ handwritten observations
- Return to office, transcribe only 15 into formal notes
- The other 25 observations influence their "feel" for the project
  but never make it into documentation
- When they leave or get reassigned, that knowledge is gone
- 73% of design revisions during construction trace back to 
  site conditions that WERE observed but NOT documented

HUMAN PATTERN #2: "The Senior Gut Check"
- Senior engineers reject designs saying "it just feels wrong"
- When pressed, they cite 5-15 specific concerns
- But never the same concerns for similar designs
- Junior engineers can't learn the pattern because it's not systematic
- One senior's "wrong feeling" about lateral load distribution
  was actually a 30-year-old memory of a bridge failure he witnessed

HUMAN PATTERN #3: "The Calculation Confidence Crisis"
- Engineers show varying confidence in hand calculations
- Some initial assumptions written in pencil, "firmed up" later
- Margin notes like "check this", "verify with Mike", "???"
- 23% of calculations contain unresolved question marks at sign-off
- Engineers sign off anyway due to deadline pressure

HUMAN PATTERN #4: "The Review Theater"
- Design reviews last 2-4 hours
- Senior reviews 60-80 pages of calculations
- Average 3.5 significant comments per review
- But 40% of comments are formatting ("use consistent units")
- Only 18% address structural engineering substance
- Reviews are perceived as quality control but function as approval theater

HUMAN PATTERN #5: "The Contractor Knowledge Gap"
- Engineers have never watched their bridges being built
- Design assumptions about constructability come from textbooks
- Contractors make field adjustments that void design assumptions
- No systematic feedback loop from construction back to design
```

---

### Running Thousands of Judgments on Human Engineering Decisions:

```python
# Analyze every design decision across 167 bridge projects
for project in bridge_projects:
    for design_decision in project.design_decisions:
        judgment = jev.ask(
            state={
                "engineer": design_decision.author,
                "engineer_experience": get_experience(design_decision.author),
                "decision_type": design_decision.type,  # material, geometry, load, etc.
                "documented_reasoning": design_decision.rationale,
                "site_conditions": project.site_data,
                "code_requirements": project.applicable_codes,
                "senior_review_outcome": design_decision.review_result,
                "construction_outcome": project.construction_result,
                "five_year_condition": project.inspection_result
            },
            questions=[
                noul("Was this design decision adequately justified by documented engineering analysis?"),
                score("How well did this decision balance safety, cost, and constructability?",
                      criteria=["Dangerously imbalanced",
                               "Biased toward single concern",
                               "Adequately balanced",
                               "Well optimized",
                               "Exemplary engineering judgment"]),
                noul("Would a different engineer with similar experience likely make a different decision?"),
                choice("What primarily drove this decision?",
                       options=["Code requirements",
                               "Site conditions",
                               "Cost optimization",
                               "Engineer familiarity/preference",
                               "Client request",
                               "Construction considerations",
                               "Unclear - no documentation"])
            ]
        )

# Analyze site visit effectiveness
for site_visit in all_site_visits:
    judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "engineer": site_visit.engineer,
            "visit_duration": site_visit.duration,
            "notebook_entries": site_visit.notebook_count,
            "formal_report_quality": assess_report(site_visit.report),
            "construction_issues": site_visit.project.construction_issues,
            "were_issues_observable": check_observability(site_visit, 
                                                          site_visit.project.construction_issues)
        },
        questions=[
            noul("Did this site visit identify conditions that later caused construction problems?"),
            noul("Were critical observations lost between field notebook and formal report?"),
            score("How effectively did this site visit inform the design process?",
                  criteria=["Visit was perfunctory, no real value",
                           "Basic observations, limited design impact",
                           "Good observations, some design influence",
                           "Thorough visit, significantly informed design",
                           "Exceptional site investigation, prevented major issues"])
        ]
    )
```

## Phase 4: pi-autoresearch Discovers Human Workflow Patterns

```python
# Experiment: What human factors predict bridge design problems?
engineering_human_factors = pi_research.design_experiment(
    hypothesis="Specific human workflow patterns predict engineering design failures",
    
    variables={
        "engineer_experience_level": ["0-2 years", "3-7 years", "8-15 years", "16+ years"],
        "site_visit_to_design_gap": ["<1 week", "1-4 weeks", "1-3 months", "3+ months"],
        "number_of_design_iterations": [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8],
        "reviewer_engineer_relationship": ["same as before", "frequent pair", "occasional", "first time"],
        "design_engineer_on_construction_site": ["never", "once", "monthly", "weekly"],
        "calculation_question_marks": [0, 1-5, 6-15, 16+],  # per design package
        "time_pressure": ["normal", "accelerated", "emergency", "impossible"]
    },
    
    outcome_metrics={
        "construction_change_orders": "count",
        "cost_overrun_percentage": "continuous",
        "design_errors_caught_in_construction": "count",
        "bridge_condition_at_5_years": ["excellent", "good", "fair", "poor"],
        "owner_satisfaction": ["very satisfied", "satisfied", "neutral", "dissatisfied"]
    },
    
    data_source="167_bridge_projects_with_full_history"
)

results = pi_research.execute(engineering_human_factors)
```

### Sample pi-autoresearch Discoveries:

```
DISCOVERY #1: "The Sweet Spot Experience Curve"
- 8-15 year engineers: 67% fewer construction problems than average
- 16+ year engineers: 45% fewer problems BUT 3x slower design time
- 0-7 year engineers: Average problems, but 40% more likely to 
  "over-engineer" (waste client money)
- 8-15 year engineers with 16+ year reviewer: BEST outcomes (83% 
  fewer problems, only 20% slower)

DISCOVERY #2: "The Two-Week Site Memory Window"
- Site visits → design start gap <2 weeks: 71% of critical site 
  conditions incorporated into design
- Gap 2-4 weeks: 54% incorporated
- Gap >4 weeks: 31% incorporated
- Engineers don't realize they're forgetting; they feel confident

DISCOVERY #3: "The Question Mark Threshold"
- Calculations with 0 unresolved "?": 4% construction problems
- 1-5 "?": 7% problems (acceptable)
- 6-15 "?": 18% problems (concerning)
- 16+ "?": 34% problems (critical)
- Engineers with 10+ "?": averaging 22% problem rate
- KEY INSIGHT: It's not experience level, it's unresolved uncertainty

DISCOVERY #4: "The Construction Site Dividend"
- Design engineers who visit construction site ≥1 time/month:
  - 58% fewer change orders
  - 41% lower cost overruns
  - 3x more likely to win next project from same client
- Engineers who never visit: worst outcomes across all metrics
- BUT: Only 23% of engineers visit sites regularly
- Reason given: "Too busy designing next bridge"

DISCOVERY #5: "The Reviewer Freshness Effect"
- Reviewer seeing design type for first time: catches 2.1x more 
  substantive issues than familiar reviewer
- Familiar reviewers fall into "confirmation bias"
- Best strategy: Rotate reviewers every 3-4 projects
- But this is counter-intuitive: "We want experienced reviewers 
  who know this bridge type!"
```

---

## Phase 5: Synthesized Human-Centered Recommendations

```python
final_recommendations = llm.synthesize(
    prompt=f"""
    Based on analysis of 167 bridge projects, 4,200 design decisions, 
    site visit notebooks, and construction outcomes:
    
    Create a "Human Workflow Optimization Plan" for this engineering firm:
    1. Address the specific human patterns discovered
    2. Work with how engineers actually think and behave
    3. Can be implemented through policy, training, and process changes
    4. Preserve engineering judgment while making it more consistent
    5. Improve knowledge transfer between engineers
    
    <findings>
    {jev_results}
    {pi_research_discoveries}
    </findings>
    """,
    output_format="practical_implementation_plan"
)
```

### Output: The Meridian Structural Engineering Human Workflow Redesign

```markdown
# Bridge Design Human Workflow Optimization Plan

## IMMEDIATE CHANGES (Policy, Not Technology)

### 1. The Two-Week Rule
**Problem:** Site knowledge decays rapidly; engineers don't realize they're forgetting
**Fix:** Design work cannot begin until 2 weeks after site visit, OR 
site visit must be repeated if gap exceeds 2 weeks.
**Rationale:** 71% → 54% → 31% site condition incorporation is unacceptable decay.

### 2. The Question Mark Audit
**Problem:** Unresolved uncertainty in calculations predicts problems
**Fix:** Before design package submitted for review:
- Count all "?", "check", "verify", "???" notations
- >5 unresolved: design not ready for review
- Engineer must resolve or document why it's acceptable
**Rationale:** 16+ question marks = 34% problem rate vs. 4% for clean calcs.

### 3. Mandatory Construction Site Visits
**Problem:** Engineers designing bridges they never see built
**Fix:** Design engineer visits construction site:
- At start of construction (see assumptions in reality)
- At critical milestones (foundation, girder placement)
- At completion (see final product)
**Rationale:** 58% fewer change orders, 41% lower overruns.

## PROCESS CHANGES (Human Behavior Modification)

### 4. The Site Notebook Transcription Protocol
**Problem:** 73% of critical site observations lost between notebook and report
**Fix:** Within 48 hours of site visit:
- Engineer dictates ALL notebook entries (voice memo)
- Junior engineer or admin transcribes verbatim
- Engineer reviews and annotates transcription
- ALL observations go into project file, not just "formal" ones
**Rationale:** The "informal" observations often prove most critical.

### 5. The Structured Gut Check
**Problem:** Senior engineers' "feelings" about designs are real but untransferable
**Fix:** When senior says "something feels wrong":
- Junior asks: "What specifically concerns you?"
- Senior must identify 3+ concrete issues (even if uncertain)
- All concerns documented, even if "probably fine"
- Concerns tracked to resolution
**Rationale:** Converts intuition into transferable knowledge.

### 6. Reviewer Rotation Program
**Problem:** Familiar reviewers miss issues due to confirmation bias
**Fix:** Reviewers rotate to different project types every 3-4 projects
- Maintain a "fresh eyes" reviewer pool
- Pair familiar + fresh reviewer on complex projects
**Rationale:** Fresh reviewers catch 2.1x more substantive issues.

## KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT (Human Systems)

### 7. The Decision Journal
**Problem:** Design rationale exists in engineer heads and margin notes
**Fix:** Every significant design decision gets 2-sentence rationale:
- Why this choice?
- What alternatives were considered?
- Written in design package, not separate document
**Rationale:** Enables knowledge transfer, reduces "why did we do this?" later.

### 8. The Construction Feedback Loop
**Problem:** No systematic learning from construction experience
**Fix:** After every project completion:
- 1-hour team debrief: "What surprised us during construction?"
- All design engineers who worked on project attend
- Notes added to searchable "Lessons Learned" binder (physical + digital)
- Monthly review: "What patterns are we seeing?"
**Rationale:** 34% of change orders trace to preventable design assumptions.

### 9. The Mentor Pairing Optimization
**Problem:** Experience level matters, but pairing matters more
**Fix:** Strategic pairing based on discovered patterns:
- 8-15 year engineer designs + 16+ year engineer reviews (complex projects)
- 16+ year engineer designs + 8-15 year engineer reviews (routine projects)
- 0-7 year engineers always paired with 8+ year for guidance
**Rationale:** Best outcomes: 83% fewer problems with only 20% time increase.
```

| Human Metric | Before | After | Change |
|-------------|--------|-------|--------|
| Construction change orders per bridge | 11 | 4.3 | **-61%** |
| Cost overruns >10% | 34% of projects | 12% | **-65%** |
| Design errors caught in construction | 8.2/project | 2.1/project | **-74%** |
| Bridges needing repair in 5 years | 8% | 3% | **-62%** |
| Engineer job satisfaction | 6.1/10 | 7.8/10 | **+28%** |
| Client repeat business | 61% | 79% | **+30%** |
| Senior engineer utilization | 115% | 89% | **-23%** (delegating appropriately) |
| Junior engineer utilization | 60% | 82% | **+37%** (better integration) |
| **Average project profit margin** | **14%** | **22%** | **+57%** |

**Next: Finance** — I'll show how this system analyzes an investment firm's human process for evaluating startup investments, where the "artifacts" are partner meeting notes, founder pitch evaluations, and investment committee deliberations.

USER
Proceed

ASSISTANT
# 💰 Finance: Startup Investment Evaluation & Portfolio Management at a Mid-Sized Venture Firm

## Real-World Context

**Cascade Ridge Capital** is a 7-partner venture capital firm in Seattle managing $340M across two funds. They make 8-12 new investments annually in Series A/B companies, plus follow-on investments. The firm has been operating for 11 years with 63 portfolio companies, 9 exits (4 meaningful returns, 5 modest), 23 active companies, 31 written-off failures.

**The human problem:** Investment decisions depend on partner pattern recognition built over decades. Partners "know" a good founder when they see one but disagree wildly with each other. Two partners evaluating identical companies reach opposite conclusions. Junior analysts produce thorough memos that partners ignore in favor of "gut feel." Founders report wildly different experiences depending on which partner leads their deal. The firm can't articulate why some investments work—when they try, the explanations don't survive scrutiny. Portfolio companies struggle or succeed in ways the partners didn't predict.

---

## Phase 1: Artifact Assimilation from Human Work Products

### What Gets Fed Into the System:

**Physical/Digital Human Artifacts:**
```
- 247 investment memos (1999-2025) with partner handwritten annotations
  - Coffee-stained pages, margin notes, crossed-out sections
  - "I like this founder but worry about TAM" with no further elaboration
  - Multiple partners' handwriting on same document showing disagreements
  
- 340 recorded partner meetings (transcribed):
  - Weekly partner meetings where deals are discussed
  - Investment committee deliberations (formal vote required)
  - "Founder concern" sessions where partners voice worries
  - Exit celebration post-mortems ("what did we see early?")
  
- 1,200 founder pitch meeting notes:
  - Junior analyst handwritten notes during pitches
  - Partner "impressions" written after (from memory, 2-48 hours later)
  - Body language observations ("founder seemed defensive when asked about churn")
  - Team dynamics notes ("CTO deferred to CEO too much")
  
- 890 email threads between partners about active deals:
  - "I'm leaning no on this but want your thoughts"
  - Multi-day deliberations with changing positions
  - Arguments about valuation, founder quality, market timing
  
- Founder reference call notes (185 total):
  - Handwritten, often incomplete
  - "Talked to former colleague - said founder is 'brilliant but difficult'"
  - Rarely followed up systematically
  
- 63 portfolio company "relationship logs":
  - Notes from board meetings
  - Partner's informal observations from founder dinners
  - Crisis intervention records ("Founder called at 11pm, possible co-founder conflict")
  - Follow-on investment decision rationale
  
- 9 exit post-mortem documents:
  - "Why Company X succeeded" (often contradicting original investment thesis)
  - "What we missed in Company Y" (failure analysis)
  - Partner individual recollections (frequently conflicting)
  
- Human resource patterns:
  - Which partner sourced which deal
  - Who championed, who opposed
  - Time spent on each deal (from calendars, not software)
  - Founder-partner chemistry assessments (subjective, from notes)
```

**Human Process Data (not software logs - human-recorded):**
```
- Deals sourced vs. pursued vs. invested: 1,400 → 247 → 63
- Partner disagreement rate on final votes: 31% (2+ partners dissenting)
- Average time from first meeting to term sheet: 47 days (range: 3-180)
- Partner "gut feel" accuracy: impossible to measure historically
- Founder satisfaction with process (from informal feedback): varies by partner
- Portfolio company founder turnover post-investment: 38% within 2 years
- Follow-on investment rate: 45% of Series A companies get Series B from Cascade
- Written-off companies where partner "knew early it wouldn't work": 71% (but didn't act)
```

## Phase 2: LLM Analyzes Human Work Patterns

```python
llm_analysis = llm.analyze(
    prompt=f"""
    Analyze this venture capital firm's human investment decision workflow for adversarial weaknesses.
    
    <artifacts>
    - 247 investment memos with partner annotations showing disagreement patterns
    - 340 recorded partner meetings revealing deliberation dynamics
    - 1,200 founder pitch meeting notes with body language and chemistry observations
    - 890 email threads showing how positions form and change
    - 185 reference call notes (often incomplete)
    - 63 portfolio relationship logs documenting post-investment dynamics
    - 9 exit post-mortems with contradictory explanations
    </artifacts>
    
    Identify:
    1. Where partner pattern recognition creates variable, unexplainable outcomes
    2. Which human evaluation signals correlate with actual investment success
    3. Where founder-investor chemistry affects decisions in undocumented ways
    4. Which "gut feelings" drive decisions without articulated reasoning
    5. Where the deliberation process introduces bias or error
    6. How knowledge about founders transfers (or fails to) between partners
    7. Where post-investment involvement patterns affect outcomes
    """,
    output_schema=VCWorkflowAnalysis
)
```

### Sample LLM Findings:

```
CRITICAL HUMAN PATTERN #1: "The Similarity Trap"
- Partners invest in founders who remind them of themselves or successful founders they've backed
- Partner A (former engineer): 73% of his investments are technical founders, 
  regardless of whether the company needs technical differentiation
- Partner B (former sales exec): Invests in "great communicators" who 
  often lack depth in the actual problem domain
- Neither pattern correlates with success better than chance

HUMAN PATTERN #2: "The Meeting Chemistry Illusion"
- Partners rate founder "presence" in meetings highly
- But meeting performance doesn't predict outcomes
- 8 of 9 successful exits had founders rated "awkward" or "defensive" in first meetings
- 5 of the worst failures had founders rated "charismatic" and "confident"
- Partners know this intellectually but can't stop weighting presence heavily

HUMAN PATTERN #3: "The Reference Call Charade"
- Partners conduct reference calls but rarely record negative information
- "Talked to former colleague - said founder is 'brilliant but difficult'" 
  → Partner interprets as positive (brilliant outweighs difficult)
- Only 12% of reference calls include critical follow-up questions
- 67% of failed companies had warning signs in reference calls that weren't pursued

HUMAN PATTERN #4: "The Dissent Suppression"
- Junior analysts produce thorough memos with concerns
- Partners dismiss: "You don't have pattern recognition yet"
- Analyst concerns were correct in 64% of cases where they disagreed with partner
- Firm has lost 3 analysts in 5 years who "felt ignored"
- One analyst's memo warned about a company that later failed, destroying $8M

HUMAN PATTERN #5: "The Portfolio Neglect Gradient"
- Partners spend disproportionate time on new deals vs. existing portfolio
- New deal evaluation: 40-60 hours per company
- Portfolio company support: 2-8 hours per month average
- Companies with high partner engagement: 71% achieve next milestone
- Companies with low engagement: 34% achieve next milestone
- Partners don't realize the correlation

HUMAN PATTERN #6: "The Narrative Rewrite"
- Post-exit, partners explain success in ways that contradict original thesis
- Original memo: "Investing because of B2B SaaS metrics"
- Post-exit story: "We always knew the consumer pivot would work"
- This prevents learning because the firm's "pattern" is fictional
```

```python
vc_human_questions = {
```

### Running Thousands of Judgments on Human Investment Decisions:

```python
# Analyze every investment decision across 63 portfolio companies
for investment in portfolio_companies:
    # Analyze the original investment decision
    investment_judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "lead_partner": investment.lead_partner,
            "partner_background": get_partner_background(investment.lead_partner),
            "investment_memo": investment.memo_with_annotations,
            "partner_meeting_notes": investment.partner_meetings,
            "reference_calls": investment.reference_notes,
            "analyst_concerns": investment.analyst_memo,
            "final_vote": investment.committee_vote,
            "dissenting_views": investment.dissent_notes,
            "outcome": investment.final_outcome,
            "outcome_details": investment.outcome_analysis
        },
        questions=[
            noul("Did the lead partner's evaluation of the founder rely more on pattern matching to previous founders or on actual evidence of capability?"),
            score("How accurately did the partner's original investment thesis predict the company's actual trajectory?",
                  criteria=["Thesis completely wrong",
                           "Thesis mostly wrong",
                           "Thesis partially correct",
                           "Thesis mostly correct",
                           "Thesis highly accurate"]),
            noul("Were warning signs present in the evaluation process that were systematically ignored or dismissed?"),
            choice("What primarily drove the final investment decision?",
                   options=["Founder quality (as perceived)",
                           "Market opportunity size",
                           "Deal momentum/FOMO",
                           "Personal connection to problem space",
                           "Analytical fundamentals",
                           "Relationship to previous successful founder",
                           "Unclear - not documented"])
        ]
    )
    
    # Analyze post-investment partner involvement
    for month in investment.monthly_involvement:
        involvement_judgment = jev.ask(
            state={
                "partner": investment.lead_partner,
                "company_stage": month.stage,
                "company_challenges": month.challenges,
                "partner_engagement": month.partner_hours,
                "founder_requests": month.founder_requests,
                "partner_responses": month.partner_responses,
                "company_progress": month.progress_metrics,
                "eventual_outcome": investment.outcome
            },
            questions=[
                noul("Was the partner's level of engagement appropriate given the company's stage and challenges?"),
                score("How valuable was the partner's contribution this month?",
                      criteria=["Harmful interference",
                               "No value added",
                               "Basic oversight only",
                               "Meaningful contribution",
                               "Decisive contribution to progress"]),
                noul("Did the partner miss opportunities to add value that would have meaningfully helped the company?")
            ]
        )

# Analyze partner deliberation patterns across all meetings
for meeting in partner_meetings:
    deliberation_judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "meeting_type": meeting.type,
            "participants": meeting.attendees,
            "duration": meeting.length,
            "concerns_raised": meeting.concerns,
            "concerns_source": meeting.concern_sources,  # partner vs analyst
            "response_to_concerns": meeting.responses,
            "decision": meeting.outcome,
            "company_outcome": get_outcome(meeting.company)
        },
        questions=[
            noul("Did this meeting surface information that improved the eventual decision quality?"),
            noul("Were concerns from junior participants given appropriate weight?"),
            score("How effectively did this deliberation process mitigate individual partner bias?",
                  criteria=["Amplified individual biases",
                           "No bias mitigation",
                           "Some challenge of assumptions",
                           "Robust challenge and synthesis",
                           "Exemplary deliberation process"])
        ]
    )
```

## Phase 4: pi-autoresearch Discovers Human Investment Patterns

```python
# Experiment: What human factors predict investment success?
vc_human_factors = pi_research.design_experiment(
    hypothesis="Specific human evaluation and engagement patterns predict venture investment outcomes",
    
    variables={
        "partner_experience_level": ["<5 years", "5-10 years", "10-20 years", "20+ years"],
        "evaluation_time_before_decision": ["<1 week", "1-4 weeks", "1-3 months", "3+ months"],
        "number_of_partner_meetings_with_founder": [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10],
        "reference_call_depth": ["none", "1-2 surface", "3-4 thorough", "5+ including negatives"],
        "partner_founder_similarity": ["very similar background", "somewhat similar", "different", "very different"],
        "dissenting_votes": [0, 1, 2, 3, 4],
        "analyst_memo_concerns_count": [0, 1-3, 4-7, 8+],
        "post_investment_engagement": ["<2 hrs/month", "2-5", "5-10", "10+"],
        "founder_partner_chemistry_rating": ["poor", "neutral", "good", "excellent"]
    },
    
    outcome_metrics={
        "investment_multiple": "continuous (0x - 10x+)",
        "company_survival_3_years": "binary",
        "next_round_raised": "binary",
        "founder_retention_2_years": "binary",
        "down_round_or_writedown": "binary"
    },
    
    data_source="63_portfolio_companies_with_full_history"
)

results = pi_research.execute(vc_human_factors)
```

### Sample pi-autoresearch Discoveries:

```
DISCOVERY #1: "The Anti-Charisma Effect"
- Founders rated "charismatic/confident" in first meetings: 
  31% achieve successful exit
- Founders rated "awkward/defensive/intense": 
  47% achieve successful exit
- Partners consistently overweight charisma (know this, can't stop)
- Charisma correlates with ability to raise follow-on but NOT with 
  ability to build sustainable company
- RECOMMENDATION: Formal "charisma discount" in evaluation

DISCOVERY #2: "The Dissent Sweet Spot"
- Investments with 0 dissenting votes: 29% success rate
- Investments with 1 dissenting vote: 52% success rate  
- Investments with 2+ dissenting votes: 33% success rate
- 1 dissent = healthy skepticism, improved decision quality
- 2+ dissent = probably real problems being overridden
- Current process treats all dissent as friction to overcome

DISCOVERY #3: "The Engagement Cliff"
- Partner engagement <2 hrs/month: 22% of companies achieve next milestone
- Partner engagement 2-5 hrs/month: 48% achieve next milestone
- Partner engagement 5-10 hrs/month: 67% achieve next milestone
- Partner engagement 10+ hrs/month: 71% achieve next milestone
- Massive jump from <2 to 2-5 hours, then diminishing returns
- Partners underestimate their impact by 3x (survey data)

DISCOVERY #4: "The Reference Call Gold Standard"
- Surface reference calls (2, positive only): 34% predict founder problems
- Thorough calls (4, including negative-seekers): 78% predict problems
- The difference: asking "What would this founder's former colleagues 
  warn me about?" vs. "Would you work with them again?"
- 67% of failed companies had reference red flags that weren't pursued

DISCOVERY #5: "The Analyst Prophecy"
- When analyst memo raised 4+ concerns: 71% of those companies failed
- When analyst raised 0-1 concerns: 38% failed
- Analysts are better predictors than partners acknowledge
- But partners dismiss: "They don't have pattern recognition"
- Firms that lost analysts who "felt ignored": their subsequent 
  predictions were correct at same rate

DISCOVERY #6: "The Similarity Penalty"
- Partners investing in founders "very similar to themselves": 
  31% success rate
- Partners investing in founders "somewhat similar": 48% success
- Partners investing in founders "different background": 55% success
- Partners know this pattern exists but can't recognize it in themselves
- One partner (former engineer) has 73% technical founders: 28% success
- Another partner (former operator) has diverse founders: 57% success
```

---

## Phase 5: Synthesized Human-Centered Recommendations

```python
final_recommendations = llm.synthesize(
    prompt=f"""
    Based on analysis of 63 portfolio companies, 247 investment memos, 
    340 partner meetings, and 11 years of investment outcomes:
    
    Create a "Human Investment Process Optimization Plan" for this VC firm:
    1. Address the specific human patterns discovered
    2. Work with how partners actually evaluate and decide
    3. Can be implemented through process changes and behavioral modifications
    4. Improve decision quality without eliminating partner judgment
    5. Better leverage the insights of junior team members
    6. Improve portfolio company outcomes through better engagement
    
    <findings>
    {jev_results}
    {pi_research_discoveries}
    </findings>
    """,
    output_format="practical_implementation_plan"
)
```

### Output: The Cascade Ridge Capital Human Investment Process Redesign

```markdown
# Venture Investment Human Process Optimization Plan

## IMMEDIATE CHANGES (Next Investment Cycle)

### 1. The Charisma Discount
**Problem:** Partners overweight founder presence in meetings; charisma doesn't predict success
**Fix:** After every founder meeting, each partner independently rates:
- "Substance" (1-10): Evidence of deep problem knowledge, specific 
  learning from failures, concrete plans
- "Presence" (1-10): Communication confidence, charisma, polish
- If Presence > Substance by 3+ points: mandatory 48-hour cooling period 
  before any commitment
**Rationale:** 47% success for "awkward" founders vs. 31% for "charismatic"

### 2. The Dissent Protocol
**Problem:** One dissenting vote improves decisions; partners treat dissent as friction
**Fix:** For every investment vote:
- Any dissenting vote triggers mandatory 10-minute "steelman" session
- Dissenting partner must articulate strongest version of their concern
- Proponents must respond specifically (not "we'll monitor that")
- If 2+ dissent: automatic second meeting required (no same-day override)
**Rationale:** 52% success with 1 dissent vs. 29% with 0 dissent

### 3. The Analyst Voice Mandate
**Problem:** Analysts predict failures at 71% accuracy when raising 4+ concerns; partners dismiss them
**Fix:** Investment committee meetings include:
- Analyst presents their concerns before partner discussion (not after)
- Each concern must be explicitly addressed in meeting minutes
- Analyst can require one follow-up question to any reference
- If analyst raises 4+ concerns: automatic additional diligence required
**Rationale:** 71% of companies with 4+ analyst concerns failed

## PROCESS CHANGES (Structural)

### 4. The Reference Call Protocol
**Problem:** Surface reference calls miss 66% of founder problems
**Fix:** Minimum 4 reference calls including:
- 1 "negative seeker": "What would this founder's former colleagues 
  warn me about?"
- 1 "failure probe": "Tell me about a time this founder failed. 
  What happened? How did they respond?"
- 1 "stress test": "How does this founder behave under extreme pressure?"
- Reference notes must include direct quotes, not paraphrases
**Rationale:** Thorough calls predict problems at 78% vs. 34% for surface calls

### 5. The Engagement Floor
**Problem:** Partner engagement <2 hrs/month correlates with 22% milestone achievement
**Fix:** Every portfolio company gets minimum 3 hours/month partner time:
- 1 hour board meeting
- 1 hour "office hours" (founder can bring anything)
- 1 hour proactive partner outreach (not waiting for founder to ask)
- Engagement tracked on simple shared sheet (not software - partner 
  initials on paper calendar in office)
**Rationale:** Jump from 22% to 48% milestone achievement at 2-5 hours/month

### 6. The Similarity Check
**Problem:** Partners invest in founders similar to themselves; this reduces success
**Fix:** Before final vote, each partner answers:
- "How similar is this founder to me in background/industry/approach?"
- "How similar to founders I've previously backed successfully?"
- If answer is "very similar" to partner but company is in partner's 
  comfort zone: another partner must lead the final evaluation
**Rationale:** "Very similar" founders: 31% success vs. "different": 55%

## KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT (Human Systems)

### 7. The Prediction Journal
**Problem:** Partners rewrite history post-exit; firm can't learn from actual patterns
**Fix:** At time of investment, each partner writes (by hand, in shared notebook):
- "I predict this company will [specific outcome] because [specific reason]"
- "The biggest risk is [specific risk]"
- "In 3 years, I expect to see [specific milestones]"
- Reviewed at exit: original predictions vs. reality
- Discrepancies discussed openly, not explained away
**Rationale:** Eliminates "narrative rewrite" that prevents learning

### 8. The Founder Pattern Library
**Problem:** Partner pattern recognition exists only in heads; can't be transferred
**Fix:** After every exit (success or failure):
- 2-hour partner debrief: "What did we see in the founder that mattered?"
- Specific behaviors documented (not "great founder" but 
  "asked for help early", "admitted mistake quickly", "changed mind with evidence")
- Library of founder behaviors → outcomes maintained
- New partners read library during onboarding
**Rationale:** Preserves institutional knowledge before partners leave

### 9. The Portfolio Ritual
**Problem:** Partners spend 40-60 hours on new deals, 2-8 hours/month on portfolio
**Fix:** Monthly "Portfolio Day" (one full day, no new deals):
- Each partner reviews all their companies
- Founder calls (not just board meetings)
- Proactive problem-solving: "What does this company need before they ask?"
- Shared learnings across portfolio companies
**Rationale:** Partners underestimate their impact 3x; structure forces engagement
```

**Next: Hardware Manufacturing** — I'll show how this system analyzes a electronics manufacturer's human process for new product introduction, where the "artifacts" are engineering change orders, production line worker feedback, and supplier relationship management.

# 🔧 Hardware Manufacturing: New Product Introduction Process at a Mid-Sized Electronics Manufacturer

## Real-World Context

**Cascade Circuit Works** is a 340-employee electronics manufacturer in Boise, Idaho producing PCB assemblies for aerospace, medical devices, and industrial automation clients. They run 3 shifts across 2 buildings, launching 8-12 new products annually with average production runs of 500-5,000 units.

**The human problem:** New product introductions consistently miss deadlines and exceed cost targets. Engineering designs "perfect" products that production can't build efficiently. Line workers discover assembly problems but information dies at shift change. Suppliers miss critical specs but purchasing doesn't learn until components fail on the line. Quality issues get "worked around" by experienced assemblers rather than fixed at root cause. When senior technicians retire, their knowledge of why processes work "that way" walks out the door.

---

## Phase 1: Artifact Assimilation from Human Work Products

### What Gets Fed Into the System:

**Physical/Digital Human Artifacts:**
```
- 14 years of New Product Introduction (NPI) records: 89 products
  - Engineering notebooks with design iterations and margin sketches
  - Design review meeting notes (engineers, production supervisor, quality)
  - First article inspection reports with handwritten inspector comments
  - Assembly work instructions (multiple versions, marked up by operators)
  - Line worker feedback forms (often completed late, sometimes blank)
  - Shift change verbal communication logs (reconstructed from interviews)
  
- 2,847 Engineering Change Orders (ECOs) with approval trails:
  - Originator notes (why change requested)
  - Implementation problems encountered
  - Cost impact estimates vs. actual costs
  - "Emergency" vs. planned classification
  - Handwritten implementation notes on floor copies
  
- Production line human observations:
  - 15,600 operator "process anomaly" reports (paper forms, varied quality)
  - Shift supervisor daily logs (handwritten, inconsistent detail)
  - Quality inspector notes on rejected assemblies
  - "Workaround" documentation (discovered through operator interviews)
  - Line balance studies (time studies with human factors notes)
  
- Supplier relationship artifacts:
  - 2,300 supplier communications (emails, meeting notes)
  - Supplier audit reports (conducted by humans, varying thoroughness)
  - "Problem supplier" lists (informal, in purchasing manager's head)
  - Component failure reports traced to supplier lots
  - Personal relationships documented through communication patterns
  
- Human resource patterns:
  - Technician experience levels by work center
  - Training records (often incomplete)
  - Cross-training matrices (who can do what)
  - Overtime patterns during NPI launches
  - Turnover by area, correlated with NPI stress periods
```

**Human Process Data (not software logs - human-recorded):**
```
- NPI timeline adherence: 34% of products launch on schedule
- Cost overruns: Average 22% over target, range 5-180%
- First pass yield on new products: 71% (target: 90%)
- ECOs per product: Average 31, range 8-127
- Line worker turnover during NPI: 18% vs. 7% normal operations
- Shift-to-shift communication effectiveness: Self-rated 4/10
- Supplier on-time delivery: 76% but varies from 45-98% by supplier
- Quality escapes to customer: 2.3% of new product batches
- Operator "workarounds" per product: Average 14, range 0-67
```

---

## Phase 2: LLM Analyzes Human Work Patterns

```python
llm_analysis = llm.analyze(
    prompt=f"""
    Analyze this electronics manufacturer's human New Product Introduction 
    workflow for adversarial weaknesses.
    
    <artifacts>
    - 14 years of NPI records with all human-generated documentation
    - 2,847 ECOs showing how changes originate and propagate
    - 15,600 operator process anomaly reports
    - Shift supervisor logs and quality inspector notes
    - Supplier communications and audit reports
    - Training records and turnover patterns
    </artifacts>
    
    Identify:
    1. Where human communication between engineering and production fails
    2. Which shift patterns correlate with quality issues
    3. Where operator knowledge creates invisible quality buffers
    4. How supplier relationships affect outcomes in undocumented ways
    5. Where NPI stress creates human performance degradation
    6. Which "workarounds" prevent root cause fixing
    7. Where training gaps create systematic failure modes
    """,
    output_schema=ManufacturingWorkflowAnalysis
)
```

### Sample LLM Findings:

```
CRITICAL HUMAN PATTERN #1: "The Shift Change Cliff"
- Day shift (6am-2pm): Senior operators, 15+ years experience average
- Swing shift (2pm-10pm): Mixed experience, 8 years average
- Night shift (10pm-6am): Junior operators, 3 years average
- Information transfer at shift change: Verbal, 5-10 minutes, no written log
- Quality escape rate: Day 0.8%, Swing 2.1%, Night 5.7%
- Root cause: Not skill difference - information about process quirks 
  doesn't transfer to night shift

HUMAN PATTERN #2: "The Engineering-Production Wall"
- Engineering designs products with 0.005" tolerance
- Production discovers actual achievable tolerance: 0.008"
- Information feedback loop: 4-6 weeks average
- 67% of ECOs originate from production discovery of design issues
- But engineering sees this as "production problems" not design problems
- One engineer's notebook: "Production always complains - build it right"
- Production supervisor's log: "Engineering lives in ideal world"

HUMAN PATTERN #3: "The Workaround Buffer"
- Experienced operators develop workarounds for design/manufacturing issues
- Examples found: Slightly different component orientation, adjusted 
  solder temperature (against spec), sequence changes in assembly
- These workarounds improve yield but are NEVER documented
- When that operator is absent: Yield drops 12-18%
- Other operators don't know the workaround exists
- 14 workarounds per product average, only 2-3 documented

HUMAN PATTERN #4: "The Supplier Relationship Blind Spot"
- Purchasing manager has 20-year relationships with key suppliers
- "Bob at Acme Components always comes through"
- When Bob retires: Acme quality drops 30%, no one understands why
- Personal relationships substitute for formal supplier management
- Audit reports exist but aren't acted on because "we know them"
- 45% of component failures trace to 3 suppliers with "good relationships"

HUMAN PATTERN #5: "The NPI Stress Cascade"
- New product launch requires overtime (60+ hours/week for key personnel)
- Week 1-2: Performance actually improves (adrenaline, focus)
- Week 3-4: Performance degrades (fatigue)
- Week 5+: Error rate increases 40% from baseline
- Key personnel turnover: 18% during NPI vs. 7% normal
- Lost knowledge when they leave: 3-6 months of product-specific learning

HUMAN PATTERN #6: "The Tribal Knowledge Fortress"
- 5 senior technicians (20+ years) know why processes work "that way"
- When asked to document: "It's too complicated to explain"
- When they retire: Process failures increase 200% for 6-9 months
- No systematic knowledge capture before retirement
- One technician's response to documentation request: "If I write it 
  down, why do they need me?"
```

---

## Phase 3: Jev Judges Human Decisions at Scale

### Question Battery for Human Manufacturing Workflow:

```python
manufacturing_human_questions = {
```

### Running Thousands of Judgments on Human Manufacturing Decisions:

```python
# Analyze every NPI launch across 89 products
for product in npi_launches:
    # Analyze the engineering-to-production transfer
    npi_judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "product": product.details,
            "engineering_effort": product.engineering_hours,
            "production_involvement_early": product.production_involvement,
            "design_review_participants": product.review_attendees,
            "work_instructions_quality": product.work_instruction_versions,
            "operator_feedback": product.operator_reports,
            "eco_count": product.eco_total,
            "timeline_performance": product.schedule_adherence,
            "cost_performance": product.cost_variance,
            "quality_outcome": product.first_pass_yield
        },
        questions=[
            noul("Did engineering involve production expertise early enough in design?"),
            score("How effectively did design intent transfer to production execution?",
                  criteria=["Complete transfer failure",
                           "Major gaps required extensive ECOs",
                           "Some gaps, manageable ECOs",
                           "Minor gaps, quick resolution",
                           "Seamless transfer, minimal ECOs"]),
            noul("Were operator insights about manufacturability incorporated before design freeze?"),
            choice("What primarily drove NPI timeline overruns?",
                   options=["Design changes required",
                           "Component/supplier issues",
                           "Production learning curve",
                           "Quality issues discovered late",
                           "Communication failures",
                           "Resource availability"])
        ]
    )
    
    # Analyze shift-specific performance across all production runs
    for shift in product.production_by_shift:
        shift_judgment = jev.ask(
            state={
                "shift": shift.details,
                "operator_experience": shift.avg_experience,
                "shift_change_process": shift.handoff_method,
                "critical_process_knowledge": shift.process_quirks,
                "knowledge_documentation": shift.documented_workarounds,
                "quality_metrics": shift.quality_data,
                "absent_operator_impact": shift.absence_impact
            },
            questions=[
                score("How effectively does this shift maintain quality standards compared to best-performing shift?",
                      criteria=["Significantly below - major quality gap",
                               "Somewhat below - noticeable quality issues",
                               "Comparable - minor variations",
                               "Equal quality standards maintained",
                               "Superior - this shift outperforms"]),
                noul("Does this shift have critical process knowledge that other shifts lack?"),
                noul("Would this shift's performance degrade significantly if key operator were absent?")
            ]
        )

# Analyze all ECOs for human factors
for eco in engineering_change_orders:
    eco_judgment = jev.ask(
        state={
            "eco": eco.details,
            "originator": eco.originator_role,
            "root_cause": eco.actual_cause,
            "discovery_method": eco.how_found,
            "implementation": eco.implementation_notes,
            "cost_impact": eco.actual_vs_estimated,
            "timeline_impact": eco.schedule_delay,
            "operator_involvement": eco.operator_input
        },
        questions=[
            choice("What category of human communication failure does this ECO represent?",
                   options=["Engineering didn't understand production capability",
                           "Production didn't communicate discovery",
                           "Supplier didn't communicate change",
                           "Cross-shift knowledge didn't transfer",
                           "Training gap in new personnel",
                           "No failure - normal iteration"]),
            noul("Could this ECO have been prevented with better human communication?"),
            score("How well did the organization learn from this ECO to prevent similar issues?",
                  criteria=["No learning - same issue recurs",
                           "Individual learning but not organizational",
                           "Documented but not implemented broadly",
                           "Process updated to prevent recurrence",
                           "Systematic prevention across product lines"])
        ]
    )
```

## Phase 4: pi-autoresearch Discovers Human Manufacturing Patterns

```python
# Experiment: What human factors predict NPI success or failure?
manufacturing_human_factors = pi_research.design_experiment(
    hypothesis="Specific human workflow patterns predict manufacturing NPI outcomes",
    
    variables={
        "engineering_production_early_involvement": ["none", "review only", "collaborative design", "co-located team"],
        "shift_experience_differential": ["minimal", "moderate", "extreme"],
        "documented_vs_undocumented_workarounds": ["all documented", "mostly documented", "mostly tribal", "all tribal"],
        "supplier_relationship_formality": ["formal", "mixed", "personal"],
        "operator_feedback incorporation_rate": ["0%", "1-25%", "26-50%", "51-75%", "76-100%"],
        "NPI_overtime_hours": ["<40", "40-50", "50-60", "60+"],
        "cross_shift_training_level": ["none", "basic", "moderate", "extensive"],
        "knowledge_capture_before_retirement": ["none", "partial", "systematic"]
    },
    
    outcome_metrics={
        "first_pass_yield": "continuous",
        "npi_timeline_adherence": "percentage",
        "eco_count": "count",
        "cost_overrun_percentage": "continuous",
        "quality_escapes_to_customer": "count",
        "operator_turnover_during_npi": "percentage",
        "time_to_stable_production": "weeks"
    },
    
    data_source="89_npi_launches_with_full_human_history"
)

# Run correlation analysis between human factors and outcomes
results = pi_research.execute(manufacturing_human_factors)

# Additional experiment: What happens when key personnel leave?
turnover_impact = pi_research.design_experiment(
    hypothesis="Loss of specific human knowledge creates predictable production degradation",
    
    variables={
        "departing_personnel_type": ["senior operator", "engineer", "supervisor", "purchasing manager"],
        "knowledge_documentation_level": ["none", "partial", "good"],
        "notice_period": ["none", "2 weeks", "1-3 months", "6+ months"],
        "successor_preparation": ["none", "basic training", "shadowing", "full transition"]
    },
    
    outcome_metrics={
        "yield_degradation_period": "weeks",
        "quality_escape_increase": "percentage",
        "cost_of_knowledge_loss": "dollars",
        "time_to_recovery": "weeks"
    },
    
    data_source="personnel_transitions_2015_2025"
)

turnover_results = pi_research.execute(turnover_impact)
```

### Sample pi-autoresearch Discoveries:

```
DISCOVERY #1: "The Early Involvement Multiplier"
- Production involvement in design phase (none): 61% first pass yield
- Production review only (late): 74% first pass yield  
- Collaborative design: 89% first pass yield
- Co-located team throughout: 91% first pass yield
- Sweet spot: Involve senior operators at concept stage, not just review
- Cost: 40 engineering hours per product
- Return: 18 fewer ECOs average, $67K saved per product

DISCOVERY #2: "The Shift Knowledge Decay Curve"
- Shift-to-shift knowledge transfer methods and yield impact:
  * Verbal only (5 min): 71% transfer effectiveness
  * Written log (15 min): 82% transfer effectiveness
  * Structured handoff (30 min): 91% transfer effectiveness
  * Cross-training + shared log: 96% transfer effectiveness
- Night shift yield with verbal only: 78% of day shift performance
- Night shift yield with full system: 97% of day shift performance
- Cost of poor transfer: $23K per month in quality issues

DISCOVERY #3: "The Tribal Knowledge Retirement Bomb"
- Senior operators (20+ years) retiring without documentation:
  * Yield drops 200% for 6-9 months
  * Quality escapes increase 350%
  * Cost: $180-340K per retirement event
- With systematic knowledge capture:
  * Yield drops 20% for 2-4 weeks
  * Quality escapes increase 50%
  * Cost: $15-30K per retirement event
- 5 senior technicians retiring in next 3 years = $1.2M risk

DISCOVERY #4: "The Supplier Relationship Trap"
- Personal relationship suppliers (no formal management):
  * On-time delivery: 76% average
  * Quality issues: 3.2% failure rate
  * When contact person leaves: 40% performance degradation
- Formally managed suppliers:
  * On-time delivery: 91% average
  * Quality issues: 0.8% failure rate
  * Contact person transition: 5% performance impact
- 3 "relationship suppliers" account for 45% of component failures
- But purchasing resists: "We've known them for 20 years"

DISCOVERY #5: "The Workaround Documentation Paradox"
- Products with all workarounds documented: 
  * Yield stability across operators: 94%
  * Absence of key operator impact: -2% yield
- Products with tribal knowledge workarounds:
  * Yield stability across operators: 71%
  * Absence of key operator impact: -15% yield
- Operators resist documentation: "Job security"
- Solution discovered: Document as "process options" not "workarounds"
  * Frame as multiple acceptable methods
  * Operator still feels valued
  * Knowledge captured without threatening job security

DISCOVERY #6: "The NPI Overtime Degradation Point"
- Overtime impact on error rates:
  * 40-50 hours/week: Baseline error rate
  * 50-60 hours/week: +15% error rate
  * 60+ hours/week: +40% error rate
  * 60+ hours for 4+ weeks: +70% error rate
- Most NPI launches require 60+ hours for 4-6 weeks
- This creates quality issues that then require more overtime
- Self-reinforcing cycle: Errors → overtime → more errors → more overtime
- Breaking the cycle: Mandatory rest periods during NPI
  * 1 day off per week minimum
  * No individual exceeds 55 hours
  * Cross-train more personnel to distribute load
```

## Phase 5: Synthesized Human-Centered Recommendations

```python
final_recommendations = llm.synthesize(
    prompt=f"""
    Based on analysis of 89 NPI launches, 2,847 ECOs, 15,600 operator reports,
    and 14 years of manufacturing outcomes:
    
    Create a "Human Manufacturing Process Optimization Plan" for this electronics manufacturer:
    1. Address the specific human patterns discovered
    2. Work within manufacturing culture and constraints
    3. Can be implemented through process and training changes
    4. Improve human knowledge capture and transfer
    5. Reduce NPI stress while improving outcomes
    
    <findings>
    {jev_results}
    {pi_research_discoveries}
    </findings>
    """,
    output_format="practical_implementation_plan"
)
```

### Output: The Cascade Circuit Works Human Process Redesign

```markdown
# Electronics Manufacturing Human Process Optimization Plan

## IMMEDIATE CHANGES (Next NPI Launch)

### 1. The Collaborative Design Requirement
**Problem:** Engineering designs without production input; 61% first pass yield
**Fix:** New product development requires:
- Senior operator (15+ years) assigned to design team from concept phase
- Minimum 4 hours/week operator involvement in design reviews
- Design cannot freeze without production sign-off on manufacturability
- "Can we build this?" question formally answered before release
**Rationale:** 89% vs. 61% first pass yield; $67K saved per product

### 2. The Structured Shift Handoff
**Problem:** Verbal shift changes lose 29% of critical knowledge; night shift yield 78% of day
**Fix:** Every shift change includes:
- 15-minute overlap between shifts
- Written log of process quirks, issues, adjustments (template provided)
- Incoming shift reads log, asks clarifying questions
- Supervisors verify handoff completion (initial the log)
**Rationale:** 91% knowledge transfer vs. 71% verbal only

### 3. The Workaround Documentation Program
**Problem:** 14 undocumented workarounds per product; yield drops 15% when key operator absent
**Fix:** Monthly "Process Options" sessions:
- Operators demonstrate alternative methods for same task
- Methods documented as "acceptable options" (not "workarounds")
- Other operators cross-trained on options
- No judgment about "right" vs. "wrong" method
**Rationale:** 94% yield stability vs. 71%; operators retain dignity

## PROCESS CHANGES (Cultural Shift)

### 4. The Supplier Formalization Initiative
**Problem:** Personal supplier relationships mask 45% of component failures
**Fix:** All suppliers formalized within 6 months:
- Audit all suppliers (even 20-year relationships)
- Formal metrics: on-time delivery, quality, responsiveness
- Dual-source critical components
- Quarterly business reviews (not just "calling Bob")
- Document institutional knowledge separate from personal relationships
**Rationale:** 91% on-time vs. 76%; 0.8% vs. 3.2% failure rate

### 5. The Knowledge Capture Countdown
**Problem:** 5 senior technicians retiring in 3 years = $1.2M risk
**Fix:** For each senior person within 5 years of retirement:
- 2 hours/week knowledge capture sessions
- Junior person shadows and documents
- Focus on "why" not just "how"
- Create training materials from captured knowledge
- Successor identified and cross-trained
**Rationale:** $15-30K transition cost vs. $180-340K

### 6. The NPI Rest Protocol
**Problem:** 60+ hour weeks for 4-6 weeks create 70% error rate increase
**Fix:** During NPI launches:
- No individual exceeds 55 hours/week
- Minimum 1 full day off per week
- Cross-train 20% more personnel to distribute load
- Mandatory "fresh eyes" review after 3 weeks (new person checks work)
**Rationale:** Break error-overtime cycle; errors cost more than schedule delay

## KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT (Human Systems)

### 7. The Cross-Shift Training Matrix
**Problem:** Night shift (3 years avg experience) lacks day shift knowledge (15+ years)
**Fix:** Quarterly cross-shift training:
- Day shift operators train night shift on process quirks
- Night shift trains day on their discoveries
- Shared documentation maintained
- Skills matrix visible (who knows what)
**Rationale:** 97% night shift performance vs. 78% of day shift

### 8. The Learning ECO System
**Problem:** Same ECO categories recur; organization doesn't learn
**Fix:** Monthly ECO review (30 minutes, all shifts represented):
- What communication failure caused this?
- What human knowledge was missing?
- How do we prevent this category?
- Document in "Lessons Learned" binder (physical, in break room)
- New employees read during onboarding
**Rationale:** 34% reduction in repeat ECO categories over 18 months

### 9. The Operator Voice Pipeline
**Problem:** Operator feedback forms ignored; 15,600 reports with minimal response
**Fix:** Daily "operator voice" review (10 minutes at shift start):
- Previous day's anomaly reports reviewed
- Response/action documented within 24 hours
- Operators informed of outcome (closure)
- Monthly "best catch" recognition (small reward)
**Rationale:** Operators provide early warning; ignoring creates workarounds
```
