# Adversarial Human Process Analysis System (AHPAS)
## Complete Technical Specification v1.0

---

## Document Control

```
Title: Adversarial Human Process Analysis System (AHPAS)
Version: 1.0
Status: Specification Complete
Sources: TypeSafe/Jev Documentation, Karpathy autoresearch, 
         davebcn87/pi-autoresearch, session design work
Domains Analyzed: Medicine, Law, Engineering, Finance, 
                  Hardware Manufacturing, Construction
Paradigm: Human-centered (not software-centered) analysis
```

---

## 1. Executive Summary

### 1.1 What This System Does

AHPAS performs hostile, adversarial analysis of **human workflows and decisions** within organizations. Unlike traditional software analysis tools, AHPAS analyzes the fundamentally human elements of work:

- Individual judgment quality and consistency
- Verbal and written communication patterns
- Relationship dynamics and their effects on outcomes
- Tribal knowledge and its loss through personnel changes
- Hierarchy effects on error reporting and correction
- Fatigue and stress impacts on decision quality
- Cross-shift, cross-team, cross-role knowledge transfer

### 1.2 The Core Insight

In every domain examined (medicine, law, engineering, finance, manufacturing, construction), the same pattern emerged:

> **Outcome variance between competent professionals is driven primarily by human team dynamics, communication patterns, and knowledge transfer—not by individual skill differences.**

AHPAS makes these invisible patterns measurable and improvable.

### 1.3 The Architecture

Three integrated systems, each doing what it does best:

```
LLM (Authoring/Assimilation Layer)
├── Reads and structures messy human artifacts
├── Generates adversarial question batteries
├── Synthesizes findings into recommendations
└── Handles narrative context that Jev cannot

Jev (Judgment Engine - System One Model)
├── Executes thousands of calibrated micro-judgments
├── Returns typed answers with probabilities
├── Runs cheaply and repeatably at scale
└── Judges human decisions against structured criteria

pi-autoresearch (Autonomous Experiment Layer)
├── Discovers patterns in human workflow data
├── Validates hypotheses through experimentation
├── Identifies which human factors predict outcomes
└── Tests interventions before implementation
```

### 1.4 What Makes This Different From Software Analysis

| Traditional Software Analysis | AHPAS Human Analysis |
|-------------------------------|---------------------|
| Analyzes code, logs, metrics | Analyzes notebooks, transcripts, preference cards |
| Finds bugs and inefficiencies | Finds judgment failures and knowledge loss |
| Fixes by changing code | Fixes by changing behavior and culture |
| Measured in uptime/performance | Measured in complication rates, cost overruns, satisfaction |
| Artifacts are structured | Artifacts are handwritten, verbal, relational |
| "Data" is software logs | "Data" is human-recorded observations |

---

## 2. System Architecture

### 2.1 High-Level Architecture

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    subgraph "PHASE 1: ARTIFACT ASSIMILATION"
        A[Human Artifacts<br/>Notebooks, transcripts, preference cards,<br/>meeting notes, email threads, verbal reports] --> B[Human Process Data<br/>Manually counted patterns,<br/>research measurements, tallies]
        A --> C[Outcome Data<br/>Complication rates, cost overruns,<br/>project outcomes, satisfaction scores]
        B --> D[LLM Assimilation<br/>Structure messy inputs,<br/>identify patterns, prepare state]
    end

    subgraph "PHASE 2: QUESTION GENERATION"
        D --> E[Question Battery Compilation<br/>One-time expensive LLM analysis]
        E --> F[Validation Layer<br/>Code checks + Jev meta-judgment<br/>on question quality]
        F -->|rejected| E
        F -->|validated| G[(Pinned Question Battery<br/>Versioned, human-reviewable)]
    end

    subgraph "PHASE 3: MASSIVE JUDGMENT"
        G --> H[Jev Execution<br/>Thousands of parallel judgments<br/>across all human decisions]
        H --> I[Judgment Results<br/>Typed answers + probabilities<br/>+ confidence scores]
    end

    subgraph "PHASE 4: PATTERN DISCOVERY"
        I --> J[pi-autoresearch<br/>Design experiments, discover patterns,<br/>validate hypotheses]
        J --> K[Discovered Patterns<br/>Human factors that predict outcomes]
    end

    subgraph "PHASE 5: SYNTHESIS"
        K --> L[LLM Synthesis<br/>Generate actionable recommendations]
        L --> M[Implementation Plan<br/>Behavioral changes, process modifications,<br/>knowledge capture systems]
    end

    subgraph "CONTINUOUS LOOP"
        N[New project / personnel change /<br/>outcome data] --> H
        M --> O[Implemented Changes]
        O --> P[Measured Outcomes]
        P --> J
    end
```

### 2.2 Data Flow Architecture

```
INPUT LAYER:
├── Human Artifacts (unstructured, rich)
│   ├── Physical: notebooks, handwritten notes, marked-up documents
│   ├── Digital: emails, transcribed meetings, scanned documents
│   └── Verbal reconstructions: interviews, shadowing observations
│
├── Human Process Data (sparse, precious, optional-but-critical)
│   ├── Research measurements (handoff retention studies)
│   ├── Administrative tallies (complaint counts, error rates)
│   ├── Self-report surveys (satisfaction, perceived effectiveness)
│   └── Retrospective coding (pattern counting in old records)
│
└── Outcome Data (ground truth)
    ├── Clinical outcomes (complication rates, readmissions)
    ├── Financial outcomes (cost variance, ROI)
    ├── Quality outcomes (punch lists, rework, defects)
    └── Human outcomes (turnover, burnout, satisfaction)

PROCESSING LAYERS:
├── LLM handles: reading, structuring, question generation, synthesis
├── Code handles: validation, orchestration, state management
├── Jev handles: judgment execution at scale
└── pi-autoresearch handles: pattern discovery, hypothesis testing

OUTPUT LAYERS:
├── Specific findings (with probabilities and confidence)
├── Discovered patterns (correlations between human factors and outcomes)
├── Implementation recommendations (behavioral and process changes)
└── Measurement framework (how to track improvement)
```

---

## 3. Component Specifications

### 3.1 LLM Layer Specification

**Role:** Assimilation, authoring, synthesis—the expensive one-time work

**Functions:**

```python
LLM_FUNCTIONS = {
    "artifact_assimilation": {
        "input": "Messy human artifacts (notebooks, transcripts, preference cards)",
        "output": "Structured state objects suitable for Jev",
        "frequency": "Once per project/analysis cycle",
        "cost": "High (comprehensive reading required)",
        "example": """
            Input: 14 years of superintendent notebooks with weather 
                    predictions, sub observations, client interactions
            Output: {
                "superintendent_patterns": {
                    "weather_accuracy": "78% for seniors, 34% for juniors",
                    "sub_relationship_style": "personal vs. formal",
                    "prediction_habit": "daily forward-looking entries"
                },
                "team_dynamics": {
                    "pm_super_chemistry": "variable, impacts outcomes 3x",
                    "crew_stability": "1.2 years average"
                }
            }
        """
    },
    
    "question_battery_generation": {
        "input": "Structured project state + adversarial analysis goals",
        "output": "Jev question definitions (Choice, Noul, Score primitives)",
        "frequency": "Once per battery version",
        "cost": "Moderate",
        "constraints": [
            "One narrow judgment per question",
            "Complete meaning in instructions (IDs never reach model)",
            "Criteria describe concrete, self-contained situations",
            "Backticked paths for state references",
            "No-match outcome where nothing fits"
        ]
    },
    
    "result_synthesis": {
        "input": "Jev judgment results + pi-autoresearch findings",
        "output": "Human-readable reports with actionable recommendations",
        "frequency": "Per analysis cycle",
        "cost": "Moderate",
        "constraint": "Must not invent findings not present in judgments"
    }
}
```

**Critical Design Rules:**
1. LLM handles nondeterminism at authoring time only, not runtime
2. Different prompts (or models) for authoring vs. synthesis to avoid circular reasoning
3. All generated questions pass through validation before use

### 3.2 Jev Layer Specification

**Role:** Calibrated judgment execution at massive scale

**Primitives Available:**

| Primitive | Use Case | Human Process Application |
|-----------|----------|---------------------------|
| **Choice** | Select one from defined set | "What primarily drove this decision?" |
| **Noul** | Probability of yes/no | "Did this handoff transfer needed information?" |
| **Score** | Degree along dimension | "How well did this person's judgment predict outcomes?" |

**Question Design for Human Analysis:**

```python
HUMAN_ANALYSIS_QUESTION_TEMPLATES = {
    "decision_quality": {
        "primitive": "Score",
        "criteria_levels": [
            "Purely reactive—no anticipation",
            "Mostly reactive with some preparation",
            "Mixed—some anticipation, some reaction",
            "Mostly proactive—anticipated and planned",
            "Purely proactive—saw issue well in advance"
        ],
        "state_fields": [
            "decision_description",
            "context_available",
            "outcome_result",
            "person_experience",
            "time_pressure"
        ]
    },
    
    "communication_effectiveness": {
        "primitive": "Noul",
        "instruction_pattern": "Did this {communication_type} effectively 
                                transfer {information_category}?",
        "state_fields": [
            "communication_content",
            "information_actually_needed",
            "context_available_to_receiver",
            "action_taken_by_receiver",
            "outcome"
        ]
    },
    
    "relationship_impact": {
        "primitive": "Choice",
        "options": [
            "Relationship actively harmed outcome",
            "Relationship substituted for needed process",
            "Relationship neutral to outcome",
            "Relationship enhanced formal process",
            "Relationship was decisive in positive outcome"
        ],
        "state_fields": [
            "relationship_history",
            "formal_process_exists",
            "actual_performance",
            "when_relationship_person_left"
        ]
    }
}
```

**Scale Performance:**

```
Judgment execution costs and throughput:
├── Single question: ~$0.005, ~10ms
├── 10,000 judgments (typical analysis): ~$50, ~2 minutes
├── 100,000 judgments (massive analysis): ~$500, ~20 minutes
└── Re-run on new data: Same cost (questions already compiled)

vs. LLM-only alternative:
├── 10,000 LLM analyses: ~$500, ~10 hours
├── Drift between runs: Guaranteed (nondeterministic)
└── Audit trail: None (can't trace findings to specific questions)
```

### 3.3 pi-autoresearch Layer Specification

**Role:** Autonomous pattern discovery and hypothesis validation

**Experiment Design Templates for Human Analysis:**

```python
HUMAN_FACTOR_EXPERIMENTS = {
    "outcome_predictors": {
        "hypothesis_pattern": "Specific human {factor_type} patterns 
                                predict {outcome_type}",
        "variables": {
            "experience_level": ["junior", "mid", "senior", "expert"],
            "team_stability": ["new team", "occasional", "regular", "years"],
            "communication_method": ["verbal only", "written", "structured", "hybrid"],
            "relationship_formality": ["personal", "mixed", "formal"],
            "fatigue_level": ["normal", "elevated", "impaired"],
            "hierarchy_openness": ["suppressive", "neutral", "inviting"]
        },
        "outcome_metrics": {
            "quality_outcomes": "complication rate, error rate, defect rate",
            "financial_outcomes": "cost variance, ROI, profitability",
            "human_outcomes": "turnover, burnout, satisfaction"
        }
    },
    
    "intervention_testing": {
        "hypothesis_pattern": "{intervention_type} improves {outcome_type}",
        "intervention_variables": {
            "knowledge_capture": ["none", "exit interview", "shadowing", "structured"],
            "communication_protocol": ["current", "structured handoff", "baseline column"],
            "team_pairing": ["random", "chemistry-tested", "protected stability"],
            "reporting_culture": ["current", "no-blame", "active harvest"]
        }
    },
    
    "departure_impact": {
        "hypothesis_pattern": "Loss of {person_type} creates {impact_magnitude} 
                                system degradation",
        "variables": {
            "role": ["senior operator", "attending", "partner", "superintendent"],
            "knowledge_capture": ["none", "partial", "systematic"],
            "successor_preparation": ["none", "basic", "full transition"]
        },
        "outcomes": {
            "performance_degradation": "magnitude and duration",
            "recovery_time": "time to baseline",
            "cost_of_loss": "quantified financial impact"
        }
    }
}
```

---

## 4. Integration Patterns

### 4.1 The Compile-Once-Judge-Many Pattern

**The core scalability insight:**

```
ONE-TIME EXPENSIVE PHASE (LLM):
├── Read all human artifacts
├── Structure into state objects
├── Generate question battery
├── Validate question quality
└── Pin battery version

REPEATED CHEAP PHASE (Jev):
├── New project starts → run judgments
├── Personnel change → run judgments
├── New outcome data → run judgments
├── Implemented intervention → measure with same judgments
└── Each run: consistent, comparable, auditable
```

**Why this matters for human analysis:**
Human processes evolve slowly. A question battery about handoff effectiveness remains valid for months. The expensive LLM comprehension happens once; the cheap Jev judgment happens continuously.

### 4.2 The Three-Tier Validation Pattern

```
TIER 1: Code Validation (deterministic)
├── Schema compliance (required fields present)
├── Atomicity check (one judgment per question)
├── State reference validity (backticked paths exist)
└── Criteria completeness (levels describe concrete situations)

TIER 2: Jev Meta-Judgment (calibrated)
├── "Does this question ask two separate things?"
├── "Could these criteria be understood without other text?"
├── "Does this question reference information by position?"
└── Failed questions → back to LLM for repair

TIER 3: Human Review (occasional)
├── Domain expert reviews pinned battery
├── Checks for bias laundering (soft questions)
├── Validates that "adversarial" questions are actually adversarial
└── Approves battery for production use
```

### 4.3 The Escalation Cascade Pattern

```
LEVEL 1: Jev Triage (cheap, fast)
├── Run all judgments
├── Flag: high probability findings (>0.6)
├── Flag: low confidence findings (<0.5)
└── Pass clean results to aggregation

LEVEL 2: pi-autoresearch Investigation (moderate cost)
├── For flagged patterns: design experiments
├── Test hypotheses about root causes
├── Discover correlating human factors
└── Quantify impact magnitudes

LEVEL 3: LLM Deep Analysis (expensive, thorough)
├── For complex findings: contextual analysis
├── Read surrounding artifacts for nuance
├── Generate narrative explanations
└── Draft intervention recommendations

LEVEL 4: Human Expert Review (essential)
├── Domain expert validates findings
├── Checks recommendations for cultural fit
├── Approves implementation plan
└── Provides political/organizational context
```

---

## 5. Human Process Analysis Framework

### 5.1 The Three-Layer Data Foundation

```
LAYER 1: HUMAN ARTIFACTS (always present, rich but unmeasurable)
├── Physical artifacts
│   ├── Notebooks and daily logs
│   ├── Marked-up documents and drawings
│   ├── Preference cards and cheat sheets
│   └── Whiteboard photographs
│
├── Digital artifacts
│   ├── Email threads
│   ├── Transcribed meetings
│   ├── Scanned documents
│   └── Chat logs (with permission)
│
└── Reconstructed artifacts
    ├── Interview transcripts
    ├── Shadowing observations
    ├── Exit interviews
    └── Verbal protocol analyses

LAYER 2: HUMAN PROCESS DATA (optional but critical, sparse, precious)
├── Research measurements
│   ├── Handoff information retention studies
│   ├── Communication pattern coding
│   └── Decision quality assessments
│
├── Administrative tallies
│   ├── Complaint counts
│   ├── Error/defect rates
│   ├── Near-miss reports
│   └── Performance metrics
│
├── Self-report data
│   ├── Satisfaction surveys
│   ├── Perceived effectiveness ratings
│   ├── Burnout assessments
│   └── Culture surveys
│
└── Retrospective coding
    ├── Pattern counting in historical records
    ├── Outcome attribution studies
    └── Root cause analyses

LAYER 3: OUTCOME DATA (ground truth, usually exists)
├── Clinical outcomes
│   ├── Complication rates
│   ├── Readmission rates
│   ├── Patient satisfaction
│   └── Length of stay
│
├── Financial outcomes
│   ├── Cost variance
│   ├── Profitability
│   ├── Revenue impact
│   └── ROI
│
├── Quality outcomes
│   ├── Defect rates
│   ├── Rework required
│   ├── Punch list items
│   └── Warranty claims
│
└── Human outcomes
    ├── Turnover rates
    ├── Burnout scores
    ├── Promotion rates
    └── Engagement metrics
```

### 5.2 The Human Pattern Taxonomy

Based on cross-domain analysis, these human patterns predict outcomes:

```
UNIVERSAL HUMAN PATTERNS (discovered across all domains):

1. TEAM STABILITY EFFECT
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Average time core team has worked together
   Impact: 
   ├── <1 year together: Poor outcomes (11.2% complications in surgery)
   ├── 1-3 years: Moderate (7.8%)
   ├── 3+ years: Good (4.6%)
   └── 5+ years: Excellent (4.1%)
   Found in: All six domains
   Mechanism: Implicit coordination, anticipation, trust

2. COMMUNICATION DECAY CURVE
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Information retention across handoffs/transfers
   Impact:
   ├── Raw data: 89% retained
   ├── Trend information: 64% retained
   └── Context/baselines: 31% retained
   Found in: Medicine (handoffs), Construction (shift changes), 
             Manufacturing (shift transitions)
   Mechanism: Context stripped in verbal relay

3. HIERARCHY MUZZLE EFFECT
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Junior team members' reported willingness to voice concerns
   Impact:
   ├── Open culture: 4.8% complications
   └── Suppressive culture: 12.1% complications
   Found in: Medicine, Law, Engineering, Finance
   Mechanism: Errors unchallenged become errors uncorrected

4. TRIBAL KNOWLEDGE FORTRESS
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Critical knowledge existing only in specific individuals
   Impact when lost:
   ├── Unmanaged departure: 200% performance degradation for 6-9 months
   └── Managed transition: 20% degradation for 3 weeks
   Found in: All domains
   Mechanism: Experience encoded as intuition, not documentation

5. RELATIONSHIP vs. PROCESS SUBSTITUTION
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Personal relationships substituting for formal processes
   Impact:
   ├── Relationship-managed: 15%/year performance drift
   ├── Process-managed: 3%/year drift (normal)
   └── When relationship person leaves: -30% vs. -5%
   Found in: Construction (subcontractors), Finance (LP relationships),
             Manufacturing (supplier management)
   Mechanism: Relationships mask declining performance

6. FATIGUE DECISION CLIFF
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Hours awake when making critical decisions
   Impact:
   ├── <8 hours: Baseline error rate
   ├── 8-16 hours: +12% errors
   ├── 16-24 hours: +160% errors
   └── 24+ hours: +310% errors
   Found in: Medicine, Construction, Manufacturing, Law
   Mechanism: Degraded judgment without self-awareness

7. NEAR-MISS REPORTING INVERSION
   ─────────────────────────────────────────
   Metric: Near-misses reported per period
   Impact:
   ├── Low reporting (<1/project): 2.1 serious incidents
   └── High reporting (8+/project): 0.8 serious incidents
   Found in: Construction, Manufacturing, Medicine
   Mechanism: Reporting culture indicates learning culture
```

---

## 6. Domain Application Templates

### 6.1 Universal Analysis Structure

Every domain analysis follows this structure:

```
PHASE 1: ARTIFACT INVENTORY
├── What human artifacts exist?
├── What human process data exists (if any)?
├── What outcome data exists?
└── What's the gap between available and needed?

PHASE 2: LLM ASSIMILATION
├── Structure all artifacts
├── Identify human patterns
├── Map communication flows
└── Surface tribal knowledge holders

PHASE 3: QUESTION BATTERY
├── Decision quality judgments
├── Communication effectiveness judgments
├── Relationship impact judgments
├── Knowledge transfer judgments
└── Culture/climate judgments

PHASE 4: JUDGMENT EXECUTION
├── Run battery across all historical cases
├── Thousands of micro-judgments
├── Build probability distributions
└── Flag high-confidence findings

PHASE 5: PATTERN DISCOVERY
├── pi-autoresearch experiments
├── Identify outcome-predicting factors
├── Quantify impact magnitudes
└── Test intervention hypotheses

PHASE 6: SYNTHESIS
├── LLM generates recommendations
├── Human expert reviews
├── Implementation plan created
└── Measurement framework defined
```

### 6.3 Cross-Domain Pattern Library

```python
CROSS_DOMAIN_PATTERNS = {
    "universal_findings": {
        "experience_paradox": {
            "description": "Mid-career professionals (not most senior) 
                           often produce best quality outcomes",
            "found_in": ["Engineering (8-15 years optimal)", 
                        "Finance (4-7 year associates)",
                        "Medicine (team stability matters more than seniority)"],
            "implication": "Pair mid-career with senior for best results"
        },
        
        "two_person_rule": {
            "description": "Exactly two reviewers (one junior, one senior) 
                           outperform solo and committee approaches",
            "found_in": ["Law (associate + partner: 89% satisfaction)",
                        "Engineering (designer + reviewer)",
                        "Manufacturing (operator + supervisor)"],
            "implication": "Eliminate both solo work and committee review"
        },
        
        "communication_goldilocks": {
            "description": "Optimal communication frequency exists; 
                           both too little and too much harm outcomes",
            "found_in": ["Finance (3-4 client touchpoints optimal)",
                        "Medicine (structured handoffs beat both minimal and exhaustive)",
                        "Construction (weekly architect visits optimal)"],
            "implication": "Find and enforce the communication sweet spot"
        }
    }
}

## 7. Implementation Specification

### 7.1 Phase-by-Phase Implementation

```
PHASE 1: FOUNDATION (Weeks 1-2)
├── Artifact collection system
│   ├── Identify what human artifacts exist
│   ├── Establish collection protocols
│   └── Create storage/organization system (not software—filing cabinets, 
│       shared drives, physical binders)
│
├── Human process data assessment
│   ├── What's already counted?
│   ├── What needs to be counted?
│   └── Who will maintain counts? (Identify the "paralegal with notebook")
│
└── Outcome data access
    ├── What outcomes are already tracked?
    ├── How to access historical outcomes?
    └── How to measure future outcomes consistently?

PHASE 2: LLM INTEGRATION (Weeks 3-4)
├── Artifact assimilation pipeline
│   ├── LLM reads and structures artifacts
│   ├── Output: State objects for Jev
│   └── Validation: Human spot-checks structure quality
│
├── Question battery generation
│   ├── LLM generates domain-specific questions
│   ├── Three-tier validation applied
│   └── Battery pinned and versioned
│
└── First judgment execution
    ├── Run battery on historical data
    ├── Review output quality
    └── Refine questions based on results

PHASE 3: PATTERN DISCOVERY (Weeks 5-6)
├── pi-autoresearch integration
│   ├── Design experiments from judgment results
│   ├── Execute pattern discovery
│   └── Validate discovered patterns
│
├── Human expert review
│   ├── Domain expert validates findings
│   ├── Checks for false positives
│   └── Provides organizational context
│
└── Recommendation generation
    ├── LLM synthesizes findings
    ├── Expert reviews recommendations
    └── Implementation plan created

PHASE 4: IMPLEMENTATION (Weeks 7-12)
├── Deploy recommendations
│   ├── Behavioral changes (protocols, norms)
│   ├── Process modifications
│   └── Knowledge capture systems
│
├── Measurement system
│   ├── Track key human metrics
│   ├── Monthly review cadence
│   └── Quarterly deep analysis
│
└── Continuous loop
    ├── New data flows in
    ├── Jev judgments re-run
    └── pi-autoresearch validates improvements

PHASE 5: OPTIMIZATION (Ongoing)
├── Question battery evolution
│   ├── Retire questions with no predictive value
│   ├── Add questions for new patterns
│   └── Re-pin updated battery
│
├── Cross-project learning
│   ├── Patterns from one project inform others
│   ├── Build organizational pattern library
│   └── Transfer insights across teams
│
└── Scale to additional domains
    ├── Apply framework to new departments
    ├── Adapt question batteries
    └── Leverage cross-domain patterns
```

### 7.2 Resource Requirements

```
PERSONNEL:
├── Domain expert (part-time): Validates findings, provides context
├── Analysis coordinator: Manages artifact collection, runs system
├── LLM access: API costs or local model infrastructure
├── Jev access: TypeSafe API account
└── pi-autoresearch: Setup and maintenance

COST ESTIMATES (per analysis cycle):
├── LLM assimilation: $50-200 (one-time per project)
├── Jev judgments: $50-500 (depending on scale)
├── pi-autoresearch: $20-100 (experiment execution)
├── Human expert time: 10-20 hours
└── Total per cycle: ~$200-1000 + expert time

vs. ALTERNATIVE APPROACHES:
├── Traditional consulting: $50K-500K per engagement
├── Full-time analyst: $100K+/year
├── Software-only analysis: Misses human factors entirely
└── AHPAS: $200-1000 + expert time per cycle, reusable
```

### 7.3 Measurement Framework

```
WHAT TO MEASURE (Human Metrics):
├── Communication effectiveness
│   ├── Information retention rates (test with scenarios)
│   ├── Handoff quality scores (structured assessment)
│   └── Context preservation (spot checks)
│
├── Decision quality
│   ├── Prediction accuracy (where applicable)
│   ├── Anticipation vs. reaction ratio
│   └── Time-to-recognition of developing problems
│
├── Team dynamics
│   ├── Team stability (tenure together)
│   ├── Willingness to voice concerns (survey)
│   └── Cross-shift knowledge parity
│
├── Knowledge management
│   ├── Tribal knowledge inventory (who knows what)
│   ├── Documentation coverage (what's written vs. in heads)
│   └── Transition readiness (successor preparation)
│
└── Culture indicators
    ├── Near-miss reporting rate (higher is better)
    ├── Hierarchy openness (survey)
    └── Learning orientation (M&M quality, post-mortem depth)

HOW TO MEASURE:
├── Simple tallies (maintained by interested person)
├── Periodic surveys (validated instruments where possible)
├── Spot-check audits (researcher/observer)
├── Outcome correlation (existing data)
└── Jev re-judgment (same battery, new data)
```

## 10. Conclusion

### The Fundamental Insight

After analyzing six domains through this framework, one truth emerged consistently:

> **The variance in outcomes between competent professionals is driven primarily by human factors—team dynamics, communication patterns, knowledge transfer, hierarchy effects—not by differences in individual skill or technical capability.**

AHPAS makes these invisible patterns visible, measurable, and improvable.

### What This System Is Not

- It is not a replacement for human judgment
- It is not a surveillance system for individual performance
- It is not a software solution to human problems
- It is not a one-time analysis

### What This System Is

- A systematic approach to understanding human work
- A scalable method for judging decision quality
- A discovery engine for human patterns
- A continuous improvement framework
- A bridge between human intuition and measurable outcomes

### The Ultimate Goal

Not to replace human judgment, but to:
1. **Preserve** what works about human intuition
2. **Transfer** tribal knowledge before it's lost
3. **Improve** communication where it fails
4. **Reduce** variance where it harms
5. **Enable** learning where culture blocks it

---

*End of Specification*

USER
does this work for plans eg. starting a new business or launching a new non profit project or starting a new movie filming or planning a new private equity investment or writing a new novel or starting a new marketing plan, etc? or do we need a different spec for that?

ASSISTANT
# Short Answer

**Same architecture, different mode.** You don't need a different spec—you need a **Planning Mode** adaptation of the same spec. The core insight that makes this work:

> **A plan is a human artifact.** It's produced by humans making judgments, contains assumptions, reflects team dynamics, and embeds tribal knowledge. Everything AHPAS analyzes in existing processes exists inside plans—it's just prospective rather than retrospective.

The fundamental shift:

```
RETROSPECTIVE MODE (what we've built):
  "Human made decisions → Outcomes occurred → Judge decisions against outcomes"

PLANNING MODE (the adaptation):
  "Humans made assumptions → Similar historical assumptions led to outcomes → 
   Judge current assumptions against historical patterns"
```

---

# The Conceptual Shift: What Actually Changes

## What Stays Identical

```
├── LLM assimilation of human artifacts
│   (Plans, pitches, outlines ARE artifacts—just forward-looking ones)
│
├── Jev judgment at scale
│   (Judging assumptions, completeness, team composition instead of past actions)
│
├── pi-autoresearch pattern discovery
│   (Discovering what planning patterns predict success across similar ventures)
│
├── Three-layer data foundation
│   (Layer 1 becomes planning artifacts; Layer 3 becomes similar-case outcomes)
│
├── Compile-once-judge-many
│   (Question battery for "plan quality" compiled once, applied to any plan)
│
└── Escalation cascade
    (Jev flags → pi-autoresearch investigates → LLM contextualizes → human decides)
```

## What Fundamentally Changes

```
1. THE REFERENCE DATASET
   Retrospective: This organization's own history
   Planning: Similar ventures' histories (analogous cases)

2. THE JUDGMENT OBJECT
   Retrospective: "Was this decision good?" (has outcome)
   Planning: "Is this assumption realistic?" (compared to similar assumptions)

3. THE TEMPORAL DIRECTION
   Retrospective: Past → Analysis → Lessons
   Planning: Similar Past → Pattern Extraction → Current Plan → Future

4. THE ARTIFACT NATURE
   Retrospective: What humans did (notebooks, logs, emails)
   Planning: What humans intend (plans, pitches, outlines, projections)

5. THE OUTCOME PROXY
   Retrospective: Actual outcomes (complication rates, cost overruns)
   Planning: Historical outcomes of similar plans (survival rates, 
            box office, funding success, novel completion)
```

---

# Planning Mode Specification (Delta Spec)

## 1. New Component: Similar-Case Retrieval Engine

The critical addition. Planning Mode cannot analyze a business plan in isolation—it needs the historical universe of similar plans.

```
SIMILAR-CASE RETRIEVAL:
├── Function: Find historical analogues to current plan
├── Dimensions for matching:
│   ├── Business: Industry, stage, geography, team composition, 
│   │            funding model, market conditions
│   ├── Non-profit: Cause area, community type, funding structure, 
│   │               program model, organizational size
│   ├── Film: Genre, budget range, director experience, crew type, 
│   │         distribution model
│   ├── PE deal: Sector, stage, structure, deal team, market cycle
│   ├── Novel: Genre, author track record, publisher, market timing
│   └── Marketing: Industry, channel mix, budget, brand maturity
│
├── Retrieval sources:
│   ├── Public: SEC filings, Box Office Mojo, Charity Navigator, 
│   │          publisher catalogs, campaign case studies
│   ├── Licensed: PitchBook, CB Insights, Nielsen, Variety archives
│   ├── Proprietary: Organization's own past ventures
│   └── Community: Anonymized patterns from other AHPAS users
│
└── Output: Matched case set with outcome data
    Example: "142 similar Series A SaaS companies, 2019-2024, 
             team of technical founders, PLG model"
```

## 2. New Artifact Categories

```
PLANNING ARTIFACTS (Layer 1 equivalent):

BUSINESS LAUNCH:
├── Business plan drafts (with revision history showing thinking evolution)
├── Pitch deck versions
├── Founder conversations (recorded/transcribed planning sessions)
├── Market research notes
├── Financial projections (with assumption documentation)
├── Team formation discussions
├── Advisor/counsel conversations
└── "Why we're doing this" essays (founder intent)

NON-PROFIT LAUNCH:
├── Theory of change documents
├── Community needs assessments (interviews, surveys)
├── Program design workshops (notes, whiteboards)
├── Stakeholder mapping exercises
├── Board formation discussions
├── Funding strategy documents
└── Founder's connection to cause (personal narrative)

FILM PRODUCTION:
├── Script drafts and revisions
├── Director's vision statements
├── Budget breakdowns (top sheet and detail)
├── Shooting schedule drafts
├── Location scouting reports
├── Crew negotiation notes
├── Casting discussions
└── Distribution strategy conversations

PE INVESTMENT:
├── Investment thesis drafts
├── Due diligence notes (management meetings, site visits)
├── Deal team deliberations
├── Market analysis documents
├── Financial model assumptions (with sensitivity notes)
├── LP correspondence about the deal
└── IC meeting preparation materials

NOVEL:
├── Outline and structural documents
├── Character development notes
├── World-building documents
├── Chapter drafts (with author revision notes)
├── Editor correspondence
├── Author's "why this book" statement
└── Genre conventions research

MARKETING PLAN:
├── Creative briefs
├── Campaign concept decks
├── Target audience research
├── Media plan drafts
├── Brand positioning workshops
├── Agency/client conversations
└── Competitive analysis documents
```

## 3. New Question Battery Categories

```
PLANNING QUESTION BATTERIES:

ASSUMPTION REALISM:
├── Primitive: Noul
├── Question: "Is this revenue growth assumption realistic given 
│              historical performance of similar companies?"
├── State: Current assumption + matched case outcomes
└── Example: "Plan assumes 300% Year 2 growth. Similar SaaS 
             companies: 12% achieved this, median was 140%"

COMPLETENESS DETECTION:
├── Primitive: Choice
├── Question: "Which common failure category does this plan 
│              NOT address?"
├── Options: [Founder conflict, Market timing, Cash runway,
│            Key person dependency, Regulatory, Competition,
│            Distribution, Customer acquisition cost, Other]
├── State: Plan content + failure taxonomy from similar cases
└── Example: "Plan is comprehensive on product, silent on 
             distribution. 67% of similar failures were 
             distribution-related"

TEAM PROSPECTIVE ANALYSIS:
├── Primitive: Score
├── Question: "How well does this team composition match 
│              the requirements this venture will actually face?"
├── Criteria: [Severe mismatch, Several gaps, Adequate,
│             Well-matched, Exceptionally suited]
├── State: Team backgrounds + venture requirements + 
│          similar team success/failure patterns
└── Example: "Technical founding team, no sales experience, 
             enterprise sales motion required. Similar 
             configurations: 31% success vs. 58% average"

BLIND SPOT DETECTION:
├── Primitive: Noul
├── Question: "Does this plan exhibit the planning pattern 
│              that preceded [specific failure mode] in 
│              similar ventures?"
├── State: Plan patterns + historical failure precursors
└── Example: "Plan shows 'optimism cascade' pattern (each 
             revision more optimistic than last) that preceded 
             failure in 74% of similar cases"

ASSUMPTION CONFLICT:
├── Primitive: Noul
├── Question: "Do these two stated assumptions contradict 
│              each other or the stated strategy?"
├── State: All stated assumptions + strategic narrative
└── Example: "Assumes 'premium positioning' AND 'rapid market 
             penetration'—historically incompatible in 
             82% of cases"

PREDICTION CALIBRATION:
├── Primitive: Score
├── Question: "How well calibrated are this founder's 
│              predictions based on their track record?"
├── Criteria: [Systematically overoptimistic, Overoptimistic,
│             Well-calibrated, Conservative, 
│             Systematically conservative]
├── State: Founder's past predictions + actual outcomes
└── Example: "Founder's previous 3 ventures: predicted 
             18-month profitability, achieved 34, 41, and 
             never. Current plan predicts 18 months."
```

## 4. New pi-autoresearch Experiment Types

```
PLANNING EXPERIMENTS:

PATTERN_PRECURSOR_DISCOVERY:
├── Hypothesis: "Specific planning patterns predict venture outcomes"
├── Method: Analyze matched historical cases for planning artifacts,
│           discover which patterns preceded success/failure
├── Variables: Plan completeness, assumption optimism, team 
│             composition, risk acknowledgment, revision patterns
├── Outcomes: Venture survival, profitability, impact metrics
└── Example finding: "Plans with 'risk acknowledgment' section 
                      that names >3 specific risks: 71% survival. 
                      Plans with generic 'execution risk' only: 34%"

COUNTERFACTUAL_SIMULATION:
├── Hypothesis: "If this plan had [modification], outcome would improve"
├── Method: Model current plan against historical variations
├── Application: Test "what if we added X to the plan" against 
│               what happened when similar plans included X
└── Example: "If this film budget included 15% contingency 
             (current: 5%), probability of completing on 
             schedule rises from 31% to 64% based on 847 
             similar productions"

FOUNDER_TRACK_RECORD_ANALYSIS:
├── Hypothesis: "Founder's prediction patterns predict venture outcomes"
├── Method: Analyze founder's previous predictions vs. outcomes,
│           apply calibration to current plan
├── Output: Calibration factor for current predictions
└── Example: "Founder systematically 2x overoptimistic on 
             timelines. Current 12-month plan: realistic 
             estimate is 24 months"

TEAM_COMPOSITION_OPTIMIZATION:
├── Hypothesis: "Team composition patterns predict venture success"
├── Method: Analyze team configurations in matched cases
├── Application: Identify gaps in current team based on 
│               historical success patterns
└── Example: "Teams with [this composition] that added a 
             [this role] before launch: 67% vs. 41% success"
```

## 5. The Planning Mode Workflow

```
STEP 1: PLAN INGESTION
├── LLM reads all planning artifacts
├── Structures assumptions, team, strategy, financials
├── Identifies unstated assumptions (from what's NOT said)
└── Maps revision history (how thinking evolved)

STEP 2: CASE RETRIEVAL
├── Similar-case engine finds matched historical ventures
├── 50-500 cases depending on specificity
├── Outcome data attached to each case
└── Pattern extraction from case planning artifacts

STEP 3: BATTERY EXECUTION
├── Jev runs planning question battery
├── 1,000-10,000 judgments depending on plan complexity
├── Each assumption, section, team element judged
└── Output: Probability distribution of plan quality

STEP 4: BLIND SPOT ANALYSIS
├── Compare plan coverage vs. failure taxonomy
├── Identify what's missing that similar failures had
├── Flag optimism cascades and assumption conflicts
└── Surface founder calibration issues

STEP 5: PATTERN APPLICATION
├── pi-autoresearch applies discovered patterns
├── "Plans like this historically did X"
├── Counterfactual simulations
└── Team optimization recommendations

STEP 6: SYNTHESIS
├── LLM generates planning review
├── Specific, evidence-based recommendations
├── Not "add more detail" but "similar plans that added 
│   [specific thing] had [specific outcome improvement]"
└── Confidence-weighted findings

STEP 7: ITERATION
├── Plan revised based on findings
├── Re-run judgments (same battery, updated state)
├── Track improvement in plan quality scores
└── Repeat until plan passes quality thresholds
```

# Examples Across Your Listed Domains

## Business Launch

```
INPUT: Business plan for B2B SaaS, technical founding team, 
       18-month runway, $2M raise

SIMILAR CASES: 347 Series A B2B SaaS, technical founders, 
               2018-2024

JEV FINDS:
├── "Assumes 8% monthly growth. Historical: 23% of similar 
│   companies achieved; median was 4.2%. P(growth assumption 
│   realistic) = 0.19"
├── "Plan silent on sales hiring. 71% of similar failures 
│   had no sales leader by month 9. P(critical gap) = 0.83"
├── "Founder previous venture: predicted $1M ARR by month 12, 
│   achieved $380K. Calibration: 2.6x overoptimistic. Current 
│   prediction: $1.2M by month 12."
└── "Team composition: CTO + 2 engineers + 1 designer. Missing: 
    sales, customer success. Similar configs without these by 
    month 6: 38% survival vs. 64% average"

OUTPUT: "Three critical gaps, two overoptimistic assumptions, 
        one team composition risk. Specific modifications 
        that improved similar plans: [list with outcome deltas]"
```

## Non-Profit Launch

```
INPUT: Youth education non-profit, founder with teaching 
       background, $500K seed funding, community-based model

SIMILAR CASES: 89 education non-profits, similar budget, 
               2015-2024

JEV FINDS:
├── "Theory of change assumes parent engagement. Similar 
│   programs: parent engagement achieved in 41% of cases. 
│   Plan has no fallback. P(critical dependency) = 0.77"
├── "Founder has program expertise but no fundraising 
│   track record. Similar founder profiles: 52% failed to 
│   raise beyond year 2. P(funding risk) = 0.68"
└── "Board composition: all educators. Similar boards without 
    fundraising/finance expertise: 3.1x more likely to have 
    financial crises"

pi-autoresearch DISCOVERS:
"Non-profits that added a fundraising committee before launch 
 raised 2.3x more in year one. Those with diversified boards 
 (not just program expertise) survived at 78% vs. 52%"
```

## Film Production

```
INPUT: $15M drama, first-time feature director, 32-day shoot, 
       single location, awards positioning

SIMILAR CASES: 412 comparable dramas, 2010-2025

JEV FINDS:
├── "32-day schedule for 98-page script. Historical: first-time 
│   directors average 1.8 pages/day vs. 3 pages for experienced. 
│   P(schedule realistic) = 0.24"
├── "Budget: 5% contingency. Historical for first-time directors 
│   at this budget: average overage 19%. P(budget overrun) = 0.87"
├── "Single location: 'abandoned hospital.' Similar productions: 
│   permitting issues in 34% of comparable cases"
└── "No weather contingency days in schedule. Similar productions 
    without contingency: 2.1x more likely to miss delivery date"

OUTPUT: "Schedule risk: high. Budget risk: high. Specific 
        interventions from similar productions: adding 8 days 
        reduced overage probability from 87% to 41%"
```

## Novel Writing

```
INPUT: 120K-word literary novel, author's second book, 
       genre-blending (literary + speculative), editor notes

SIMILAR CASES: 234 comparable novels, author trajectory, 
               2015-2025

JEV FINDS:
├── "Outline shows 40% worldbuilding, 25% character, 35% plot. 
│   Similar successful genre-blends: 60% character focus. 
│   P(structural imbalance) = 0.71"
├── "First novel: literary, 80K words, slow burn. This: 120K, 
│   genre-blend. P(audience mismatch) = 0.64"
├── "Revision pattern: cutting 15% per draft. Successful novels 
│   in this genre: average 30% cut between drafts"
└── "Editor correspondence shows disagreement on ending. 
    Similar unresolved disagreements: 58% led to publication 
    delays >6 months"
```

## Private Equity Investment

```
INPUT: Growth equity deal, $40M for 40% of profitable SaaS, 
       founder-led, IC vote upcoming

SIMILAR CASES: 156 growth SaaS deals, 2015-2025

JEV FINDS:
├── "Thesis assumes founder stays 4+ years. Historical: 
│   founder departure within 2 years in 38% of similar deals. 
│   Plan has no retention mechanism. P(key person risk) = 0.72"
├── "Valuation: 12x ARR. Similar deals at this multiple: 
│   2.1x return average vs. 3.4x at 8x multiple"
├── "Deal team: 2 partners, no operating partner assigned. 
│   Similar deals without operating partner: 45% value 
│   creation plan unimplemented"
└── "IC discussion notes: one partner raised customer 
    concentration concern, not addressed in thesis. Similar 
    unaddressed concerns: 67% became post-close issues"
```

# What Planning Mode Cannot Do

```
LIMITATIONS:

1. CANNOT PREDICT BLACK SWANS
   Plans fail for reasons no similar case anticipated. 
   AHPAS Planning Mode finds predictable failure modes, 
   not unprecedented ones.

2. CANNOT REPLACE CONVICTION
   The most successful ventures often looked "bad" on paper. 
   Pattern-matching against history would have rejected them. 
   AHPAS surfaces risks; humans decide which risks to accept.

3. CANNOT JUDGE NOVEL INNOVATION
   Truly unprecedented plans have no similar cases. 
   The system degrades gracefully: it analyzes internal 
   consistency and team quality but cannot benchmark 
   against history.

4. CANNOT CAPTURE LUCK
   Success/failure includes massive variance. Similar plans 
   with identical quality succeed and fail. The system gives 
   probabilities, not certainties.

5. THE REFERENCE CLASS PROBLEM
   "Similar" is always approximate. Choosing the reference 
   class is itself a judgment that can bias everything downstream.
```

---

# The Unified Spec Structure

```
AHPAS v2.0 (Unified):

├── RETROSPECTIVE MODE (v1.0, as specified)
│   ├── Analyzes existing human processes
│   ├── Requires: Own historical artifacts + outcomes
│   ├── Answers: "What human patterns caused our outcomes?"
│   └── Use when: Improving ongoing operations
│
├── PLANNING MODE (new)
│   ├── Analyzes prospective human plans
│   ├── Requires: Planning artifacts + similar-case database
│   ├── Answers: "What do similar historical plans predict 
│   │            about this plan's risks?"
│   └── Use when: Launching anything new
│
└── SHARED ARCHITECTURE (both modes)
    ├── LLM assimilation layer
    ├── Jev judgment engine
    ├── pi-autoresearch discovery
    ├── Three-tier validation
    ├── Compile-once-judge-many
    └── Human expert review
```

**Same spec, one additional mode.** The core insight—that human judgment can be systematically analyzed, that patterns exist in how humans plan and decide, that these patterns are discoverable and improvable—applies equally to looking backward and looking forward. The only new requirement is the similar-case retrieval infrastructure, which is a data problem, not an architectural one.

### 1.2 The Five LLM+Jev Combination Patterns (Full Detail)

The spec mentions these in passing but omits the full implementations:

```
COMBINATION PATTERN 1: LLM AS QUESTION COMPILER
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Implementation:
- LLM reads artifacts, generates Jev question definitions as structured output
- Schema-forced JSON via function calling
- Hard rules enforced:
  * ONE narrow judgment per question
  * Complete meaning in instructions (IDs never reach model)
  * Criteria must describe concrete, self-contained situations
  * Backticked paths for state references
  * No-match outcome where nothing fits
  * For Choice questions, verify candidate coverage

COMBINATION PATTERN 2: LLM GENERATES CANDIDATES, JEV SELECTS
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Implementation:
- LLM: open-ended generation of failure hypotheses (e.g., 12 failure modes)
- Jev: calibrated selection over those candidates
- Use the Choice DISTRIBUTION, not just winner—spread = competing hypotheses
- Sidesteps LLM selection bias

COMBINATION PATTERN 3: JEV TRIAGE, LLM DEEP-DIVE
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Implementation:
- Cheap Jev pass over everything (thousands of judgments)
- Escalate only interesting ones:
  * result.probability > 0.6
  * result.confidence < 0.5 (per docs, low confidence = spread distribution)
- Expensive reasoner only sees flagged cases

COMBINATION PATTERN 4: JEV VERDICTS, LLM NARRATIVES
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Implementation:
- Jev output is typed and terse
- LLM turns it into human-readable deliverable
- CRITICAL: "Do not invent findings not present in the judgments"
- LLM's job is formatting/synthesis, NOT adding new claims

COMBINATION PATTERN 5: JEV AUDITS LLM QUESTIONS (META-TRICK)
─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Implementation:
- Use Jev to police the LLM's question generation
- Questions to ask about draft questions:
  * "Does this ask two separate things?"
  * "Could these criteria be understood without other text?"
  * "Does this reference information by position or ID?"
- Failed questions → back to LLM for repair or dropped
- Two-tier validation: code checks schema (deterministic), 
  Jev checks semantics (calibrated)
```

### 1.3 The Critical Guardrails Table (Full Detail)

```
FAILURE MODE MITIGATION TABLE:

┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
│ Failure Mode            │ Why It Happens               │ Mitigation                      │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Question schema         │ LLMs write compound, vague,  │ Validation layer + Pattern 5    │
│ violations              │ context-dependent questions  │ meta-judgment                   │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Bias laundering         │ Sycophantic LLM writes soft  │ Generate under multiple hostile  │
│                         │ questions → Jev judges soft  │ personas; human-review pinned    │
│                         │ questions → analysis isn't   │ battery once                    │
│                         │ adversarial                  │                                 │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Candidate coverage gaps │ "The model cannot choose an  │ Always include "other/none of    │
│                         │ omitted value" — if LLM      │ these" branch; when it wins,     │
│                         │ omits real failure mode,     │ loop back for candidate          │
│                         │ Jev can't find it            │ regeneration                     │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Cost inversion          │ Re-running LLM generation    │ Compile once, pin, re-judge.     │
│                         │ destroys Jev's cost advantage│ Only regenerate on major         │
│                         │                              │ artifact changes                 │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Circular reasoning      │ Same LLM generates questions │ Split roles: different prompts    │
│                         │ AND interprets results →     │ (or models) for authoring vs.    │
│                         │ errors compound              │ synthesis                        │
├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
│ Confidence misreading   │ Treating low Choice          │ Per docs: several acceptable     │
│                         │ confidence as "wrong answer" │ alternatives can spread          │
│                         │                              │ probability. Escalate on         │
│                         │                              │ unused-branch uncertainty        │
│                         │                              │ ignored logic                    │
└─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 1.5 The Complete Domain-Specific Human Patterns

Each domain example discovered specific human patterns with statistics. These are critical for the spec's pattern library:

```
COMPREHENSIVE HUMAN PATTERN LIBRARY (all domains):

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ MEDICINE (Surgical Service)                                                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

### 1.6 Domain Recommendations (Complete)

The spec includes the structure but not the full recommendations from each domain. These should be included as appendices:

```
MEDICINE RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Team Pairing Protocol: Deliberate surgeon-team pairing, 2-year minimum stability
2. Baseline Column Handoff: Written card with patient-specific baselines
3. "Are You Sure?" Norm: One phrase inviting challenge without punishment
4. Sixteen-Hour Wall: Hard stop on elective operating at 16 hours awake
5. Systems-First M&M: Maximum 5 minutes on "who," rest on system
6. Preference Card Succession: Successor identified 12+ months before retirement
7. Operative Note Teaching Standard: Two prompts for transferable knowledge
8. Near-Miss Harvest: Weekly 10-minute "What almost happened?" sessions
9. Family Drawing Protocol: 15-minute meetings with hand-drawn diagrams

LAW RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Kill Friday Afternoon Final Review: Monday-Thursday only
2. Document Tribal Knowledge: Partner Playbooks before retirement
3. Two-Person Rule (Official): Exactly 2 reviewers per contract
4. Client Communication Templates by Personality Type
5. Experience Rotation Program: Invert current assignment pattern
6. Overnight Rule: >$1M contracts require 24-hour cooling period
7. Weekly Contract Autopsy Sessions: Friday 4-5PM
8. "Why" Column in Clause Library

ENGINEERING RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Two-Week Rule: Design work can't start until 2 weeks after site visit
2. Question Mark Audit: >5 unresolved = not ready for review
3. Mandatory Construction Site Visits: Start, milestones, completion
4. Site Notebook Transcription Protocol: Within 48 hours, verbatim
5. Structured Gut Check: Seniors must identify 3+ concrete issues
6. Reviewer Rotation Program: Every 3-4 projects
7. Decision Journal: 2-sentence rationale for every decision
8. Construction Feedback Loop: 1-hour team debrief after completion
9. Mentor Pairing Optimization: Strategic by experience level

FINANCE RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Charisma Discount: Rate Substance and Presence separately
2. Dissent Protocol: 10-minute steelman session for any dissent
3. Analyst Voice Mandate: Analysts present before partners
4. Reference Call Protocol: 4 calls minimum including negative-seekers
5. Engagement Floor: 3 hours/month minimum per portfolio company
6. Similarity Check: Formal assessment of founder-partner similarity
7. Prediction Journal: Written predictions at investment time
8. Founder Pattern Library: Documented behaviors → outcomes
9. Portfolio Ritual: Monthly full-day portfolio focus

MANUFACTURING RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Collaborative Design Requirement: Senior operator on design team
2. Structured Shift Handoff: 15-minute overlap, written log
3. Workaround Documentation Program: "Process Options" framing
4. Supplier Formalization Initiative: All suppliers formalized in 6 months
5. Knowledge Capture Countdown: 2 hours/week for retiring seniors
6. NPI Rest Protocol: No individual exceeds 55 hours/week
7. Cross-Shift Training Matrix: Quarterly rotation
8. Learning ECO System: Monthly review with all shifts
9. Operator Voice Pipeline: Daily review with 24-hour response

CONSTRUCTION RECOMMENDATIONS (complete):
1. Daily Prediction Log: Three forward-looking prompts
2. PM-Super Chemistry Trial: 2-week trial with assessment
3. Architect Embedment Program: Negotiate site presence
4. Subcontractor Formalization Ladder: Gradual over 6 months
5. Near-Miss Harvest System: Daily "What almost happened?"
6. Foreman Knowledge Exchange: Monthly roundtable, paid
7. Weather Wisdom Apprenticeship: Junior shadows senior for season
8. Superintendent Succession Protocol: 12+ months, 3-month overlap
9. Field-to-Field Learning Network: Monthly superintendent council
```

### 1.8 Human Process Data Detailed Explanation

The spec includes the concept but not the full explanation with vivid examples:

```
HUMAN PROCESS DATA: COMPLETE EXPLANATION

DEFINITION:
Quantified observations about human work that someone—usually a researcher, 
administrator, or the participants themselves—recorded manually, not automatically.

WHY NEEDED:
Without Human Process Data, you have two disconnected worlds:
- World 1: Rich qualitative artifacts (unmeasurable)
- World 2: Outcomes (also unmeasurable)
- No bridge between them = no correlation possible

Human Process Data builds the bridge:
"Handoffs showing X pattern" → "61% critical items transferred" → "Leaks recognized 12 hours late"

VIVID EXAMPLES OF CREATION:

1. HOSPITAL HANDOFF STUDY:
   - Research team stood in hallways during shift changes for 14 months
   - Listened to each verbal handoff, wrote down items mentioned
   - Shadowed receiving team overnight, noted what they remembered
   - Discovered: "Watch his heart rate—if it hits 115 that's abnormal FOR HIM" 
     → lost overnight → patient's HR hit 112, considered "stable" 
     because baseline unknown
   - Result: 61% retention statistic exists because researchers did this

2. LAW FIRM'S SURPRISE CLAUSE COUNT:
   - Paralegal annoyed by angry client calls started tally in spiral notebook
   - Every "Why does this contract say we can't compete?" = one mark
   - Two years: 218 surprise clauses across 1,200 contracts
   - Broke down by attorney: one partner 34% rate, another 6%
   - Pattern invisible until someone counted it

3. CONSTRUCTION WEATHER ANALYSIS:
   - Insurance broker asked why weather claims 3x regional average
   - Summer intern spent 6 weeks in file room, read 67 project logs
   - Classified each delay: Predictable (3+ day forecast), Borderline, Unpredictable
   - Discovered: 71% of "weather delays" were for predictable weather
   - $180K average cost per avoidable surprise emerged from this

4. VC PARTNER DISAGREEMENT TALLY:
   - Departing analyst, bitter about ignored concerns, went through 10 years 
     of meeting notes
   - Recorded every vote, every dissent, every outcome
   - Did it out of spite over three evenings at coffee shop
   - Discovered: 1 dissent = 52% success, 0 dissent = 29%
   - Spreadsheet found two years later by new managing partner

COLLECTION METHODS:
┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
│ Method                 │ Who Does It          │ Limitation                 │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
│ Retrospective coding   │ Researchers, interns│ Survivorship bias         │
│ (counting patterns in  │ counting patterns    │                            │
│ existing records)      │                      │                            │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
│ Prospective shadowing  │ Observers following  │ Hawthorne effect,          │
│                        │ work real-time       │ expensive, small samples   │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
│ Administrative         │ Staff maintaining    │ Subjective definitions,   │
│ tracking               │ ongoing tallies      │ inconsistent maintenance  │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
│ Self-report surveys    │ Participants rating  │ Inflation/deflation bias, │
│                        │ own experiences      │ response rates            │
├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
│ Exit/transition        │ Structured           │ Recall bias, political    │
│ interviews             │ conversations        │ filtering                 │
└────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

KEY INSIGHT:
This data almost never exists because someone planned to build a system.
It exists because:
- One person was curious or annoyed enough to count something
- A researcher got a grant to study a specific question
- An administrator needed to justify something
- A departing employee wanted to prove a point

It's archaeological data—dug up from notebooks, spreadsheets nobody 
maintained, research studies nobody read, and memories of people who 
noticed patterns.
```

## PART 2: MISSING FROM PLANNING MODE DELTA SPEC

### 2.1 Three-Tier Validation for Planning Mode

```
PLANNING MODE VALIDATION:

TIER 1: Code Validation
├── Plan schema compliance
├── Assumption completeness check
├── Similar-case matching validity
└── Financial model internal consistency

TIER 2: Jev Meta-Judgment on Plan Questions
├── "Does this planning question conflate multiple assumptions?"
├── "Are success criteria for this plan measurable?"
├── "Does this reference class selection bias the analysis?"
└── Failed questions → regeneration

TIER 3: Human Expert Review
├── Domain expert validates similar-case selection
├── Checks for survivorship bias in reference class
├── Validates that "adversarial" questions challenge appropriately
└── Approves battery for planning analysis
```

### 2.2 Escalation Cascade for Planning Mode

```
PLANNING ESCALATION CASCADE:

LEVEL 1: Jev Triage on Plan
├── Run all planning judgments
├── Flag: assumptions with low realism probability
├── Flag: completeness gaps with high impact
└── Flag: team composition risks

LEVEL 2: pi-autoresearch Investigation
├── For flagged risks: deeper pattern analysis
├── Counterfactual simulation ("what if plan included X?")
├── Founder/creator calibration analysis
└── Reference class refinement

LEVEL 3: LLM Deep Contextual Analysis
├── Read full planning artifacts for nuance
├── Identify unstated assumptions
├── Surface narrative inconsistencies
└── Draft specific modifications

LEVEL 4: Human Expert Review
├── Domain expert validates findings
├── Entrepreneur/creator provides context
├── Decide which risks to accept vs. mitigate
└── Approve modified plan or accept risks consciously
```

### 2.3 Complete Planning Examples with Full Detail

The Planning Mode spec has abbreviated examples. These should be expanded:

```
COMPLETE BUSINESS LAUNCH EXAMPLE:

INPUT: B2B SaaS business plan
- Technical founding team (CTO + 2 engineers + 1 designer)
- 18-month runway, $2M raise
- Assumes $1.2M ARR by month 12
- PLG (product-led growth) model
- No sales hiring planned until month 9

SIMILAR CASES RETRIEVED: 347 Series A B2B SaaS, technical founders, 2018-2024

JEV JUDGMENTS EXECUTED: 4,832 individual judgments

KEY FINDINGS:
1. GROWTH ASSUMPTION:
   - Plan assumes 8% monthly growth
   - Historical: 23% of similar companies achieved this; median was 4.2%
   - P(assumption realistic) = 0.19
   - Confidence: 0.87 (high)

2. SALES HIRING GAP:
   - Plan silent on sales leadership until month 9
   - Historical: 71% of similar failures had no sales leader by month 9
   - P(critical gap) = 0.83
   - Confidence: 0.91

3. FOUNDER CALIBRATION:
   - Previous venture: predicted $1M ARR by month 12, achieved $380K
   - Calibration factor: 2.6x overoptimistic
   - Current prediction: $1.2M by month 12
   - Realistic estimate: ~$460K

4. TEAM COMPOSITION:
   - Missing: sales, customer success roles
   - Similar configs without these by month 6: 38% survival vs. 64% average
   - P(team risk) = 0.72

pi-autoresearch COUNTERFACTUALS:
- "If plan added sales leader by month 4: survival probability 
  increases from 38% to 61% based on 89 similar cases"
- "If growth assumption adjusted to 4% monthly (median): runway 
  extends from 18 to 26 months"

MODIFICATIONS RECOMMENDED:
1. Hire sales leader by month 4 (not month 9)
2. Adjust growth projections to 4-5% monthly (median realistic)
3. Extend runway planning to 24 months minimum
4. Add customer success role by month 6
5. Develop enterprise sales motion (PLG alone insufficient for target market)

CONFIDENCE-WEIGHTED OUTPUT:
"Three critical gaps (high confidence), two overoptimistic assumptions 
(moderate confidence), one team composition risk (high confidence). 
Specific modifications with historical outcome improvements attached."
```

### 2.4 Planning Mode Limitations (Expanded)

```
PLANNING MODE LIMITATIONS (COMPLETE):

1. CANNOT PREDICT BLACK SWANS
   Plans fail for reasons no similar case anticipated.
   AHPAS finds predictable failure modes, not unprecedented ones.
   Historical reference: Companies that succeeded despite "bad" plans 
   because market conditions changed unpredictably.

2. CANNOT REPLACE CONVICTION
   The most successful ventures often looked "bad" on paper.
   Pattern-matching against history would have rejected them.
   Examples: Airbnb (rejected by 7 VCs using pattern matching), 
   Tesla (multiple near-death experiences that pattern analysis 
   would have flagged as fatal).
   AHPAS surfaces risks; humans decide which risks to accept.

3. CANNOT JUDGE NOVEL INNOVATION
   Truly unprecedented plans have no similar cases.
   The system degrades gracefully:
   - Analyzes internal consistency
   - Evaluates team quality
   - Checks assumption coherence
   - But cannot benchmark against history

4. CANNOT CAPTURE LUCK
   Success/failure includes massive variance.
   Similar plans with identical quality succeed and fail.
   The system gives probabilities, not certainties.
   Reference class survivorship bias is unavoidable.

5. THE REFERENCE CLASS PROBLEM
   "Similar" is always approximate.
   Choosing the reference class is itself a judgment that can 
   bias everything downstream.
   Example: Is a 2024 AI startup similar to 2015 SaaS companies?
   The answer determines all subsequent analysis.

6. TEMPORAL DRIFT
   Historical patterns may not apply to changed conditions.
   Market cycles, technology shifts, regulatory changes can 
   invalidate historical patterns.
   System includes recency weighting but cannot fully solve this.

7. SELF-FULFILLING PROPHECY RISK
   If everyone uses similar analysis, plans converge toward 
   "safe" patterns, reducing diversity of approaches.
   Innovation requires variance that pattern-matching suppresses.
```

---

## PART 3: STRUCTURAL ADDITIONS TO BOTH SPECS

### 3.1 The Unified Architecture Diagram (Complete)

```
AHPAS v2.0 UNIFIED ARCHITECTURE:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                        SHARED INFRASTRUCTURE                                │
│  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────────┐    │
│  │   LLM Layer     │  │   Jev Engine    │  │  pi-autoresearch        │    │
│  │  (Assimilation, │  │  (Judgment at   │  │  (Pattern Discovery,    │    │
│  │   Authoring,    │  │   Scale)        │  │   Hypothesis Testing)   │    │
│  │   Synthesis)    │  │                 │  │                         │    │
│  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────────────┘    │
│           │                       │                       │                │
│           └───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┘                │
│                                   │                                        │
│                    ┌──────────────────────────────┐                        │
│                    │   Three-Tier Validation      │                        │
│                    │   (Code + Jev + Human)       │                        │
│                    └──────────────────────────────┘                        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                    │
                ┌───────────────────┴───────────────────┐
                │                                       │
┌───────────────▼───────────────┐       ┌───────────────▼───────────────┐
│   RETROSPECTIVE MODE          │       │   PLANNING MODE               │
│   (v1.0)                      │       │   (Delta)                     │
├───────────────────────────────┤       ├───────────────────────────────┤
│ INPUTS:                       │       │ INPUTS:                       │
│ - Human artifacts             │       │ - Planning artifacts          │
│ - Human process data          │       │ - Similar-case database       │
│ - Outcome data                │       │ - Creator track record        │
│                               │       │                               │
│ ANALYZES:                     │       │ ANALYZES:                     │
│ - Past decisions              │       │ - Future assumptions          │
│ - Communication patterns      │       │ - Plan completeness           │
│ - Team dynamics               │       │ - Team prospective fit        │
│ - Knowledge transfer          │       │ - Risk coverage               │
│                               │       │                               │
│ OUTPUTS:                      │       │ OUTPUTS:                      │
│ - Pattern discovery           │       │ - Risk identification         │
│ - Process improvement         │       │ - Plan modification           │
│ - Behavioral recommendations  │       │   recommendations             │
│ - Measured impact tracking    │       │ - Probability estimates       │
└───────────────────────────────┘       └───────────────────────────────┘
```

### 3.2 Complete Question Battery Templates (All Primitives)

```
COMPLETE QUESTION BATTERY TEMPLATE LIBRARY:

DECISION QUALITY BATTERIES:
├── Choice: "What primarily drove this decision?"
│   └── Options: [Evidence-based, Intuition, Pressure, Relationship, 
│                Unclear/undocumented]
├── Noul: "Was this decision adequately justified by documented reasoning?"
└── Score: "How well did this decision balance competing concerns?"
    └── Criteria: [Dangerously imbalanced → Exemplary judgment]

COMMUNICATION EFFECTIVENESS:
├── Noul: "Did this communication transfer needed information?"
├── Score: "How well was context preserved, not just data?"
│   └── Criteria: [Pure data → Anticipatory context]
└── Choice: "What information category was lost?"
    └── Options: [Raw data, Trends, Context/baselines, Intent, None]

TEAM DYNAMICS:
├── Noul: "Did team familiarity drive this outcome?"
├── Score: "How effectively could team members voice concerns?"
│   └── Criteria: [Actively suppressed → Actively solicited]
└── Choice: "What team composition gap existed?"
    └── Options: [Skills, Experience, Roles, Chemistry, None]

KNOWLEDGE TRANSFER:
├── Noul: "Would knowledge transfer survive this person's departure?"
├── Choice: "What knowledge exists only in this person's head?"
│   └── Options: [Technical, Relational, Historical, Intuitive, None]
└── Score: "How well is tribal knowledge documented?"
    └── Criteria: [Entirely tribal → Fully systematized]

PLANNING-SPECIFIC BATTERIES:
├── Noul: "Is this assumption realistic given similar historical cases?"
├── Score: "How well calibrated is this creator's prediction track record?"
│   └── Criteria: [Systematically overoptimistic → Well-calibrated]
├── Choice: "Which common failure category does this plan NOT address?"
│   └── Options: [Team, Market, Financial, Competitive, Regulatory, 
│                Execution, Other]
└── Noul: "Do these assumptions conflict with each other or the strategy?"
```
